So for any
\delta>0
, we can find
C
depending only on
\delta
such that

\displaystyle\frac{\int_{x:\left\|x\right\|\geq C}\exp(f(x))dx}{\int_{x}\exp(f(x))dx}\leq\delta.

∎

Proof of Proposition
3
.

We apply Lemma
1
to the case when
\delta=\frac{1}{2}
and obtain the corresponding constant
C>0
. It is easy to see that for any
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\displaystyle\exp(-\max_{k=1,\ldots,K}\|\alpha_{k}\|\|\widehat{\theta}-\theta\|)(I(\widehat{\theta})-I_{d})\leq(I(\theta)-I_{d})\leq\exp(\max_{k=1,\ldots,K}\|\alpha_{k}\|\|\widehat{\theta}-\theta\|)(I(\widehat{\theta})-I_{d}).

(S.118)

Now let

\displaystyle g_{1}(\theta)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}(\theta-\widehat{\theta})^{\mathrm{T}}[I_{d}+\exp(-C\max_{k=1,\ldots,K}\|\alpha_{k}\|)(I(\widehat{\theta})-I_{d})](\theta-\widehat{\theta})+f(\widehat{\theta}),

\displaystyle g_{2}(\theta)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}(\theta-\widehat{\theta})^{\mathrm{T}}[I_{d}+\exp(C\max_{k=1,\ldots,K}\|\alpha_{k}\|)(I(\widehat{\theta})-I_{d})](\theta-\widehat{\theta})+f(\widehat{\theta})

be strictly concave function with maximizer as
\widehat{\theta}
and maximum value as
f(\widehat{\theta})
. Then for any
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\|\theta-\widehat{\theta}\|\leq C
, by (
S.118
) we have

\displaystyle-\nabla^{2}g_{1}(\theta)\leq I(\theta)\leq-\nabla^{2}g_{2}(\theta).

(S.119)

Since the maximizers and maximum values are matched for
f,g_{1},g_{2}
, by (
S.119
) we have

\displaystyle\int_{\theta:\|\theta-\widehat{\theta}\|\leq C}\exp(g_{2}(\theta))d\theta\leq\int_{\theta:\|\theta-\widehat{\theta}\|\leq C}\exp(f(\theta))d\theta\leq\int_{\theta:\|\theta-\widehat{\theta}\|\leq C}\exp(g_{1}(\theta))d\theta.

(S.120)

By the definition of
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
, it is easy to prove that
-\nabla^{2}g_{1}(\theta)\succeq I_{d}
and
-\nabla^{2}g_{2}(\theta)\succeq I_{d}
for any
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then by the choice of
C
and (
S.120
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{\int\exp(f(\theta))d\theta}{\int\exp(g_{2}(\theta))d\theta}\geq\frac{\int_{\theta:\|\theta-\widehat{\theta}\|\leq C}\exp(f(\theta))d\theta}{2\int_{\theta:\|\theta-\widehat{\theta}\|\leq C}\exp(g_{2}(\theta))d\theta}\geq\frac{1}{2},

\displaystyle\frac{\int\exp(f(\theta))d\theta}{\int\exp(g_{1}(\theta))d\theta}\leq\frac{2\int_{\theta:\|\theta-\widehat{\theta}\|\leq C}\exp(f(\theta))d\theta}{\int_{\theta:\|\theta-\widehat{\theta}\|\leq C}\exp(g_{1}(\theta))d\theta}\leq 2.

(S.121)

Moreover, by the definition of
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
we have

\displaystyle\int(2\pi)^{-d/2}\exp(g_{1}(\theta))d\theta

\displaystyle=\exp(f(\widehat{\theta}))[\operatorname{det}(I_{d}+\exp(-C\max_{k=1,\ldots,K}\|\alpha_{k}\|)(I(\widehat{\theta})-I_{d}))]^{-1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\exp(f(\widehat{\theta})+\frac{Cd}{2}\max_{k=1,\ldots,K}\|\alpha_{k}\|)(\operatorname{det}(I(\widehat{\theta}))^{-1/2},

\displaystyle\int(2\pi)^{-d/2}\exp(g_{2}(\theta))d\theta

\displaystyle=\exp(f(\widehat{\theta}))[\operatorname{det}(I_{d}+\exp(C\max_{k=1,\ldots,K}\|\alpha_{k}\|)(I(\widehat{\theta})-I_{d}))]^{-1/2}

\displaystyle\geq\exp(f(\widehat{\theta})-\frac{Cd}{2}\max_{k=1,\ldots,K}\|\alpha_{k}\|)(\operatorname{det}(I(\widehat{\theta})))^{-1/2}.

(S.122)

Then by (
S.2.5
) and (
S.2.5
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\exp(-\frac{Cd}{2}\max_{k=1,\ldots,K}\|\alpha_{k}\|)\leq\frac{\int(2\pi)^{-d/2}\exp(f(\theta))d\theta}{\exp(f(\widehat{\theta}))/\sqrt{\operatorname{det}(I(\widehat{\theta}))}}\leq 2\exp(\frac{Cd}{2}\max_{k=1,\ldots,K}\|\alpha_{k}\|).

Since constant
C
does not depend on the choice of
\xi
, the result is proved.
∎

S.2.6
Proof of Proposition
4

Proof of Proposition
4
.

We prove part (1) and (2) of Proposition
4
simultaneously and prove the uniqueness and continuity of canonical projection in the end. We first consider the case when
\left\{\eta_{n}\right\}\subseteq span\left\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{K}\right\}\triangleq\mathcal{H}_{0}
. The first-order equation corresponding to
\theta_{n}
is as

\displaystyle-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\exp(\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}\theta_{n})\alpha_{k}+\eta_{n}=\theta_{n}.

(S.123)

If
\theta_{n}=0,i.o.
, then (
S.123
) implies that
\eta=0
, which contradicts with our assumption. So we assume WLOG that
\theta_{n}\neq 0
and denote
l_{n}=\left\|\theta_{n}\right\|
,
\epsilon_{n}=\theta_{n}/l_{n}
. Then equation (
S.123
) turns into

\displaystyle-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\exp(l_{n}\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}\epsilon_{n})\alpha_{k}+\eta_{n}=l_{n}\epsilon_{n}.

(S.124)

Since
\left\|\epsilon_{n}\right\|=1
for any
n
, we assume WLOG that
\epsilon_{n}\rightarrow\epsilon
where
\epsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
has norm 1, otherwise we can make arguments on a subsequence.

Step 1:
We prove that

l_{n}\rightarrow\infty
.

If the is not the case, we assume WLOG that
l_{n}\rightarrow l<\infty
. Then we have

\displaystyle\eta_{n}=l_{n}\epsilon_{n}+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\exp(l_{n}\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}\epsilon_{n})\alpha_{k}\rightarrow l\epsilon+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\exp(l\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}\epsilon)\alpha_{k},

where the right-hand side is finite. This implies that
\eta=0
, which contradicts with our assumption.

Step 2:
We divide the problem into three cases regarding the sign of
\underset{k=1,\ldots,K}{\max}\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon
.

Case 1:
\underset{k=1,\ldots,K}{\max}\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon<0
.

Since
l_{n}
goes to infinity and
\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}\epsilon_{n}\rightarrow\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}\epsilon<0
for any
k=1,\ldots,K
, by (
S.124
) we have

\displaystyle\left\|\eta_{n}-l_{n}\epsilon_{n}\right\|=\left\|\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\exp(l_{n}\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}\epsilon_{n})\alpha_{k}\right\|\rightarrow 0.

This indicates that
\epsilon=\eta/\left\|\eta\right\|
. So we have
\underset{k=1,\ldots,K}{\max}\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\eta<0
, then we choose an empty set to satisfy the conditions in part (1), i.e.,
\mathcal{H}_{\eta}=\emptyset
and
\mathcal{H}_{\eta}^{\perp}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Moreover, since
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\eta_{n}/{n}=\eta
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\theta_{n}}{n}=\eta=P_{\mathcal{H}_{\eta}^{\perp}}\eta

and

\displaystyle f_{n}(\theta_{n})=

\displaystyle-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\exp(l_{n}\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}\epsilon_{n})+\eta_{n}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta_{n}-\frac{1}{2}\theta_{n}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta_{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle o(1)+n^{2}\left(\eta+o(1)\right)^{\mathrm{T}}\left(\eta+o(1)\right)-\frac{n^{2}}{2}\left(\eta+o(1)\right)^{\mathrm{T}}\left(\eta+o(1)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\left\|P_{\mathcal{H}_{\eta}^{\perp}}\eta\right\|^{2}+o(1)\right)n^{2}.

Case 2:
\underset{k=1,\ldots,K}{\max}\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon>0
.

Assume that
\left\{\alpha_{k_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{k_{m}}\right\}=\left\{\alpha_{k}:\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon=\underset{m=1,\ldots,K}{\max}\alpha_{m}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon,k=1,\ldots,K\right\}
. By multiplying equation (
S.124
) by
\epsilon
, we have

\displaystyle-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\exp(l_{n}\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}\epsilon_{n})\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon+\eta_{n}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon=l_{n}\epsilon_{n}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon.

(S.125)

Since
l_{n}\rightarrow\infty
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\sum_{p=1}^{m}\omega_{k}\exp(l_{n}\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k_{p}}\epsilon_{n})\alpha_{k_{p}}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon}{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\exp(l_{n}\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}\epsilon_{n})\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon}=1.

(S.126)

For any
p=1,\ldots,m
, since
l_{n}\rightarrow\infty
and
\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k_{p}}\epsilon_{n}\rightarrow\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k_{p}}\epsilon>0
, we have
l_{n}\ll\exp(l_{n}\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k_{p}}\epsilon_{n})
. So by (
S.126
), we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{l_{n}\epsilon_{n}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon}{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\exp(l_{n}\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}\epsilon_{n})\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\sum_{p=1}^{m}\omega_{k_{p}}\exp(l_{n}\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k_{p}}\epsilon_{n})\alpha_{k_{p}}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon}{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\exp(l_{n}\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}\epsilon_{n})\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon}\times\frac{l_{n}\epsilon_{n}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon}{l_{n}}\times\frac{l_{n}}{\sum_{p=1}^{m}\omega_{k_{p}}\exp(l_{n}\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k_{p}}\epsilon_{n})\alpha_{k_{p}}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

(S.127)

So by (
S.125
), (
S.126
) and (
S.2.6
), we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\sum_{p=1}^{m}\omega_{k_{p}}\exp(l_{n}\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k_{p}}\epsilon_{n})\alpha_{k_{p}}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon}{\eta_{n}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon}=

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\sum_{p=1}^{m}\omega_{k_{p}}\exp(l_{n}\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k_{p}}\epsilon_{n})\alpha_{k_{p}}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon}{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\exp(l_{n}\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}\epsilon_{n})\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon}\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\exp(l_{n}\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}\epsilon_{n})\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon}{\eta_{n}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\sum_{p=1}^{m}\omega_{k_{p}}\exp(l_{n}\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k_{p}}\epsilon_{n})\alpha_{k_{p}}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon}{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\exp(l_{n}\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}\epsilon_{n})\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon}\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\exp(l_{n}\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}\epsilon_{n})\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon}{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\exp(l_{n}\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}\epsilon_{n})\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon-l_{n}\epsilon_{n}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1,

which indicates that
l_{n}=o(n)
. Divide equation (
S.125
) by
n
, and combine with (
S.126
), we have

\displaystyle\sum_{p=1}^{m}\frac{\omega_{k_{p}}\exp(l_{n}\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k_{p}}\epsilon_{n})}{n}\alpha_{k_{p}}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon=\eta^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon+o(1).

Since
\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k_{p}}\epsilon>0
, it is easy to see that
\omega_{k_{p}}\exp(l_{n}\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k_{p}}\epsilon_{n})/{n}
is bounded for any
p=1,\ldots,m
. Then we assume WLOG that for any
p=1,\ldots,m
,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\omega_{k_{p}}\exp(l_{n}\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k_{p}}\epsilon_{n})}{n}=\gamma_{k_{p}},

(S.128)

where
\gamma_{k_{1}},\ldots,\gamma_{k_{m}}
are nonnegative constants. Then for any
\alpha_{m}\notin\left\{\alpha_{k_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{k_{m}}\right\}
, there holds
\alpha_{m}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon_{n}\leq\underset{k=1,\ldots,K}{\max}\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon-\delta
for
n
large, where
\delta>0
is a positive constant. So we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\omega_{m}\exp(l_{n}\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{m}\epsilon_{n})}{n}\leq\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\omega_{m}\exp(l_{n}\left[\underset{k=1,\ldots,K}{\max}\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon-\delta\right])}{n}=0.

Then by dividing first-order equation (
S.124
) by
n
, we deduce that
\eta=\sum_{p=1}^{m}\gamma_{k_{p}}\alpha_{k_{p}}
. Since
\eta\neq 0
, at least one of
\gamma_{k_{1}},\ldots,\gamma_{k_{m}}
is strictly positive, which indicates that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{l_{n}}{\log n}=\frac{1}{\underset{k=1,\ldots,K}{\max}\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon}.

(S.129)

So we choose all vectors in
\left\{\alpha_{k}:k=1,\ldots,K\right\}
to satisfy the condition in part (1). Here
\mathcal{H}_{\eta}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{\eta}=\emptyset
. Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\theta_{n}}{n}=0=P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{\eta}}\eta.

Since by (
S.129
), for any
\delta>0
, there holds
\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\exp(l_{n}\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}\epsilon_{n})=o(\exp((1+\delta)\log n))=o(n^{1+\delta})
, we have

\displaystyle f_{n}(\theta_{n})=o(n^{2})=\left(\left\|P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{\eta}}\eta\right\|^{2}+o(1)\right)n^{2}.

Case 3:
\underset{k=1,\ldots,K}{\max}\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon=0
.

Define
{f}^{(0)}_{n}=f_{n}
,
{\theta}_{n}^{(0)}=\theta_{n}
and define

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}^{(0)}_{n}(\theta)=-\sum_{k:\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon=0}\omega_{k}\exp(\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}\theta)+\eta_{n}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta-\frac{1}{2}\theta^{\mathrm{T}}\theta=f_{n}^{(0)}(\theta)+\sum_{k:\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon<0}\omega_{k}\exp(\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}\theta).

Denote the unique maximum point of
\widetilde{f}_{n}^{(0)}
by
\widetilde{\theta}_{n}^{(0)}
. Then the first order equations for
\theta_{n}^{(0)}
and
\widetilde{\theta}_{n}^{(0)}
are

\displaystyle\nabla\widetilde{f}^{(0)}_{n}({\theta}_{n}^{(0)})+\sum_{k:\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon<0}\omega_{k}\exp(\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}{\theta}_{n}^{(0)})\alpha_{k}

\displaystyle=\nabla f^{(0)}_{n}(\theta_{n}^{(0)})=0,

\displaystyle\nabla\widetilde{f}^{(0)}_{n}(\widetilde{\theta}_{n}^{(0)})

\displaystyle=0.

Since
\nabla^{2}\widetilde{f}_{n}^{(0)}(\theta)\preceq-I_{d}
for any
\theta
, by Taylor expansion, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\theta_{n}^{(0)}-\widetilde{\theta}_{n}^{(0)}\right\|\leq

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{2}\widetilde{f}^{(0)}_{n}(\theta_{n}^{\ast})(\theta_{n}^{(0)}-\widetilde{\theta}_{n}^{(0)})\right\|=\left\|\nabla\widetilde{f}^{(0)}_{n}({\theta}_{n}^{(0)})-\nabla\widetilde{f}^{(0)}_{n}(\widetilde{\theta}_{n}^{(0)})\right\|=\left\|\sum_{k:\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon<0}\exp(l_{n}\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}\epsilon_{n})\alpha_{k}\right\|\rightarrow 0

since
l_{n}
goes to infinity. Since
\left\|\theta_{n}^{(0)}-\widetilde{\theta}_{n}^{(0)}\right\|\rightarrow 0
, it is easy to prove that
\sum_{k:\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon<0}\exp(\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}\widetilde{\theta}_{n}^{(0)})\rightarrow 0
. Then we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}_{n}^{(0)}(\widetilde{\theta}_{n}^{(0)})-f_{n}^{(0)}(\theta_{n}^{(0)})\leq

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}_{n}^{(0)}(\widetilde{\theta}_{n}^{(0)})-f_{n}^{(0)}(\widetilde{\theta}_{n}^{(0)})=\sum_{k:\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon<0}\exp(\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}\widetilde{\theta}_{n}^{(0)})\rightarrow 0,

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}_{n}^{(0)}(\widetilde{\theta}_{n}^{(0)})-f_{n}^{(0)}(\theta_{n}^{(0)})\geq

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}_{n}^{(0)}(\theta_{n}^{(0)})-f_{n}^{(0)}(\theta_{n}^{(0)})=\sum_{k:\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon<0}\exp(\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}\theta_{n}^{(0)})\rightarrow 0,

which implies that
\widetilde{f}_{n}^{(0)}(\widetilde{\theta}_{n}^{(0)})-f_{n}^{(0)}(\theta_{n}^{(0)})\rightarrow 0
.
Denote
\mathcal{H}_{1}=span\left\{\alpha_{k}:k=1,\ldots,K,\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon=0\right\}
and denote

\displaystyle{f}_{n}^{(1)}(\theta)=-\sum_{k:\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon=0}\omega_{k}\exp(\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}\theta)+\left(P_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}\eta_{n}\right)^{\mathrm{T}}\theta-\frac{1}{2}\theta^{\mathrm{T}}\theta.

We then plug
{\theta}_{n}^{(1)}\triangleq\widetilde{\theta}^{(0)}_{n}-P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{1}}\eta_{n}
into the gradient of
{f}_{n}^{(1)}
:

\displaystyle\nabla{f}_{n}^{(1)}({\theta}_{n}^{(1)})=

\displaystyle-\sum_{k:\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon=0}\omega_{k}\exp(\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}(\widetilde{\theta}_{n}^{(0)}-P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{1}}\eta_{n}))+P_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}\eta_{n}-(\widetilde{\theta}_{n}^{(0)}-P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{1}}\eta_{n})

\displaystyle=-\sum_{k:\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon=0}\omega_{k}\exp(\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}\widetilde{\theta}_{n}^{(0)})+\eta_{n}-\widetilde{\theta}_{n}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=0,

where the last step is due to the first order equation for
\widetilde{f}^{(0)}_{n}(\widetilde{\theta}_{n}^{(0)})
. This implies that
{\theta}_{n}^{(1)}
is the maximum point for
{f}_{n}^{(1)}
. Moreover, we have

\displaystyle{f}_{n}^{(1)}({\theta}_{n}^{(1)})=

\displaystyle-\sum_{k:\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon=0}\omega_{k}\exp\left(\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}\left(\widetilde{\theta}^{(0)}_{n}-P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{1}}\eta_{n}\right)\right)+\left(\eta_{n}-P_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\perp}}\eta_{n}\right)^{\mathrm{T}}\left(\widetilde{\theta}^{(0)}_{n}-P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{1}}\eta_{n}\right)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\left(\widetilde{\theta}^{(0)}_{n}-P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{1}}\eta_{n}\right)^{\mathrm{T}}\left(\widetilde{\theta}^{(0)}_{n}-P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{1}}\eta_{n}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{k:\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon=0}\omega_{k}\exp\left(\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}\widetilde{\theta}^{(0)}_{n}\right)+\eta_{n}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\theta}^{(0)}_{n}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\widetilde{\theta}^{(0)}_{n}\right)^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\theta}^{(0)}_{n}+\frac{1}{2}\left\|P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{1}}\eta_{n}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}_{n}^{(0)}(\widetilde{\theta}_{n}^{(0)})+\frac{1}{2}\left\|P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{1}}\eta_{n}\right\|^{2}.

We then prove that at least one vector among
\left\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{K}\right\}
is eliminated in the procedure from
f_{n}^{(0)}
to
f_{n}^{(1)}
, which is equivalent to proving that
\alpha_{1}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon=\ldots=\alpha_{K}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon=0
can not happen. If it is the case, we multiply (
S.124
) by
\epsilon
to get

\displaystyle l_{n}\epsilon_{n}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon=\eta_{n}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\exp(l_{n}\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}\epsilon_{n})\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon=\eta_{n}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon.

Since
\eta_{n}\in span\left\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{K}\right\}
, we have
\eta_{n}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon=0
. Since
l_{n}\rightarrow\infty
and
\epsilon_{n}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon\rightarrow\epsilon^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon=1
, we have
l_{n}\epsilon_{n}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon\rightarrow\infty
. This leads to contradiction. So in this procedure, at least one vector is eliminated. Then we apply the same procedure on
f_{n}^{(1)}
to discuss which of the three cases it falls into. This procedure will stop over finite steps, i.e, falls into Case 1 or 2 over finite steps. In this process, we get a sequence of
\theta_{n}^{(0)},\ldots,\theta_{n}^{(r)}
,
\widetilde{\theta}_{n}^{(0)},\ldots,\widetilde{\theta}_{n}^{(r-1)}
and
\mathcal{H}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{H}_{r}
such that for
p=0,\ldots,r-1
,

\displaystyle\theta_{n}^{(p)}-\widetilde{\theta}_{n}^{(p)}

\displaystyle=o(1),

\displaystyle\theta_{n}^{(p+1)}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\theta}_{n}^{(p)}-P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{p+1}}\left(P_{\mathcal{H}_{p}}\eta_{n}\right),

\displaystyle f_{n}^{(p+1)}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{k:\alpha_{k}\in\mathcal{H}_{p}}\exp(\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}\theta)+n\left(P_{\mathcal{H}_{p}}\eta\right)^{\mathrm{T}}\theta-\frac{1}{2}\theta^{\mathrm{T}}\theta.

(S.130)

Denote
\theta_{n}^{(p)}/\left\|\theta_{n}^{(p)}\right\|=\epsilon_{n}^{(p)}\rightarrow\epsilon^{(p)}
. The procedure will fall into one of the two cases in the last step:

Case 3.1:
\underset{k:\alpha_{k}\in\mathcal{H}_{r}}{\max}\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon^{(r)}<0
.

Then by the proof in Case 1,
\theta_{n}^{(r)}=P_{\mathcal{H}_{r}}\eta_{n}+o(1)
. Combine this with (
S.2.6
), we have

\displaystyle\theta^{(0)}_{n}=o(1)+\sum_{p=0}^{r-1}P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{p+1}}\left(P_{\mathcal{H}_{p}}\eta_{n}\right)+P_{\mathcal{H}_{r}}\eta_{n}=\eta_{n}+o(1).

This implies that
\epsilon^{(0)}\triangleq\epsilon\propto\eta
and
\epsilon^{(p)}\propto P_{\mathcal{H}_{p}}\eta,~p=1,\ldots,r
. For any
k=1,\ldots,K
, there exists
p=0,\ldots,r
such that
\alpha_{k}\in\mathcal{H}_{p}\setminus\mathcal{H}_{p+1}
(define
\mathcal{H}_{r+1}=\emptyset
), then

\displaystyle 0>\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}\epsilon^{(p)}\propto\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}P_{\mathcal{H}_{p}}\eta=\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}\left(\eta-P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{p}}\eta\right)=\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}\eta.

So for any
k=1,\ldots,K
,
\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}\eta<0
, which indicates that the problem should fall into Case 1.

Case 3.2:
\underset{k:\alpha_{k}\in\mathcal{H}_{r}}{\max}\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon^{(r)}>0
.

By the proof in Case 2,
\theta_{n}^{(r)}=O(\log(n))
. Moreover,

\displaystyle P_{\mathcal{H}_{r}}\eta

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\gamma_{k_{j}}\alpha_{k_{j}},~~~~\gamma_{k_{1}},\ldots,\gamma_{k_{m}}\geq 0,

where
\alpha_{k_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{k_{m}}
are the remaining vectors after
r
steps and
\mathcal{H}_{r}=span\left\{\alpha_{k_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{k_{m}}\right\}
. Combine this with (
S.2.6
), we have

\displaystyle\theta_{n}=O(\log n)+\sum_{p=0}^{r-1}P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{p+1}}P_{\mathcal{H}_{p}}\eta_{n}=P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{r}}\eta_{n}+o(n).

Then
\epsilon^{(0)}\triangleq\epsilon\propto P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{r}}\eta
and
\epsilon^{(p)}\propto P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{r}}\eta-P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{p}}\eta
. So for any
k\in\left\{1,\ldots,K\right\}\setminus\left\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{m}\right\}
, there exists
p=0,\ldots,r-1
such that
\alpha_{k}\in\mathcal{H}_{p}\setminus\mathcal{H}_{p+1}
. Then we have

\displaystyle 0>\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}\epsilon^{(p)}\propto\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}\left(P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{r}}\eta-P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{p}}\eta\right)=\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{r}}\eta,

which implies that condition in part (1) is satisfied by choosing
\mathcal{H}_{\eta}
as
\mathcal{H}_{r}
.

Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle f_{n}^{(0)}(\theta_{n}^{(0)})=

\displaystyle\sum_{p=0}^{r-1}\left[\left(f_{n}^{(p)}({\theta}_{n}^{(p)})-\widetilde{f}_{n}^{(p)}(\widetilde{\theta}_{n}^{(p)})\right)+\left(\widetilde{f}_{n}^{(p)}(\widetilde{\theta}_{n}^{(p)})-f_{n}^{(p+1)}({\theta}_{n}^{(p+1)})\right)\right]+f_{n}^{(r)}(\theta_{n}^{(r)})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{p=0}^{r-1}\left[o(1)+\frac{1}{2}\left\|P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{p+1}}\left(P_{\mathcal{H}_{p}}\eta_{n}\right)\right\|^{2}\right]+o(n^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left\|P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{r}}\eta_{n}\right\|^{2}+o(n^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{2}\left\|P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{\eta}}\eta\right\|^{2}+o(1)\right)n^{2}.

If not all vectors of
\left\{\eta_{n}\right\}
are in
\mathcal{H}_{0}
, then we define
\widetilde{\eta}_{n}=P_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}\eta_{n}
and
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\widetilde{\eta}_{n}/n=\widetilde{\eta}
. Then by the previous proof, there exists
\left\{\alpha_{k_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{k_{m}}\right\}\subseteq\left\{\alpha_{k}:~k=1,\ldots,K\right\}
such that the conditions in (1) are satisfied. Denote
\mathcal{H}=span\left\{\alpha_{k_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{k_{m}}\right\}
, then
P_{\mathcal{H}}\eta_{n}=P_{\mathcal{H}}\left(P_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}\eta\right)=P_{\mathcal{H}}\widetilde{\eta}_{n}
, which implies that
P_{\mathcal{H}}\eta=P_{\mathcal{H}}\widetilde{\eta}
. Furthermore, for
k\notin\left\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{m}\right\}
,

\displaystyle\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}}\eta=

\displaystyle\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\left(P_{\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\perp}}\eta+P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}}\left(P_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}\eta\right)\right)=\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}}\widetilde{\eta}<0.

So
\left\{\alpha_{k_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{k_{m}}\right\}
also satisfies the conditions for
\eta
.

Moreover, define

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}_{n}(\theta)=-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\exp(\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}\theta)+\left(P_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}\eta_{n}\right)^{\mathrm{T}}\theta-\frac{1}{2}\theta^{\mathrm{T}}\theta

and its unique maximum point as
\widetilde{\theta}_{n}
. We use similar method to prove that
\widetilde{\theta}_{n}=\theta_{n}-P_{\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\perp}}\eta_{n}
and
\widetilde{f}_{n}(\widetilde{\theta}_{n})=f_{n}(\theta_{n})-\frac{1}{2}\left\|P_{\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\perp}}\eta_{n}\right\|^{2}
.
Since
P_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}\eta_{n}\in\mathcal{H}_{0}
for any
n
, by previous proof we have
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\widetilde{\theta}_{n}}{n}=P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}}\widetilde{\eta}
and
\widetilde{f}_{n}(\widetilde{\theta}_{n})=\left(\frac{1}{2}\left\|P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}}\widetilde{\eta}\right\|^{2}+o(1)\right)n^{2}
. This implies that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{{\theta}_{n}}{n}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\widetilde{\theta}_{n}+P_{\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\perp}}\eta_{n}}{n}=P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}}\widetilde{\eta}+P_{\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\perp}}\eta=P_{\mathcal{H}_{\eta}^{\perp}}\left(P_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}\eta\right)+P_{\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\perp}}\eta=\eta-P_{\mathcal{H}_{\eta}}\left(P_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}\eta\right)=P_{\mathcal{H}_{\eta}^{\perp}}\eta

and

\displaystyle f_{n}(\theta_{n})=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{2}\left\|P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}}\widetilde{\eta}\right\|^{2}+o(1)\right)n^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\left\|P_{\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\perp}}\eta_{n}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{2}\left\|P_{\mathcal{H}_{\eta}^{\perp}}\left(P_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}\eta\right)\right\|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\left\|P_{\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\perp}}\eta\right\|^{2}+o(1)\right)n^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(\left\|P_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}\eta\right\|^{2}-\left\|P_{\mathcal{H}_{\eta}}\left(P_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}\eta\right)\right\|^{2}+\left\|P_{\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\perp}}\eta\right\|^{2}+o(1)\right)n^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(\left\|\eta\right\|^{2}-\left\|P_{\mathcal{H}_{\eta}}\eta\right\|^{2}+o(1)\right)n^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{2}\left\|P_{\mathcal{H}_{\eta}^{\perp}}\eta\right\|^{2}+o(1)\right)n^{2}.

Finally, we prove the uniqueness and continuity of canonical projection.

Uniqueness:
Denote function

\displaystyle g_{n}(\theta)=

\displaystyle-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\exp(\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta)+n\eta^{\mathrm{T}}\theta-\frac{1}{2}\theta^{\mathrm{T}}\theta.

and denote the unique maximum point of
g_{n}
by
{\theta}_{n}
. If there exists
\left\{\alpha_{k_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{k_{m}}\right\}\subseteq\left\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{K}\right\}
such that

\displaystyle P_{\mathcal{H}_{\eta}}\eta

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\gamma_{k_{j}}\alpha_{k_{j}},~~~~\gamma_{k_{1}},\ldots,\gamma_{k_{m}}\geq 0

\displaystyle\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}P_{\mathcal{H}_{\eta}^{\perp}}\eta

\displaystyle<0,~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\forall k\in\left\{1,\ldots,K\right\}\setminus\left\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{m}\right\}

where
\mathcal{H}_{\eta}=span\left\{\alpha_{k_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{k_{m}}\right\}
, then we define

\displaystyle\widehat{g}_{n}(\theta)=

\displaystyle-\sum_{k:\alpha_{k}\in\mathcal{H}_{\eta}}\exp(\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta)+n\eta^{\mathrm{T}}\theta-\frac{1}{2}\theta^{\mathrm{T}}\theta,

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{n}(\theta)=

\displaystyle-\sum_{k:\alpha_{k}\in\mathcal{H}_{\eta}}\exp(\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta)+n\left(P_{\mathcal{H}_{\eta}}\eta\right)^{\mathrm{T}}\theta-\frac{1}{2}\theta^{\mathrm{T}}\theta,

and denote the maximizers of
\widehat{g}_{n}
and
\widetilde{g}_{n}
by
\widehat{\theta}_{n}
,
\widetilde{\theta}_{n}
, respectively. Follow similar proof as in part (2), we have
\theta_{n}-\widehat{\theta}_{n}=o(1)
and
\widetilde{\theta}_{n}=\widehat{\theta}_{n}-nP_{\mathcal{H}_{\eta}^{\perp}}\eta
. Denote
\|\widetilde{\theta}_{n}\|={l}_{n}
,
\widetilde{\theta}_{n}/{l}_{n}=\epsilon_{n}\rightarrow\epsilon
. We then prove that
\max_{k:\alpha_{k}\in\mathcal{H}_{\eta}}\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon>0
. If this is not the case, then
\max_{k:\alpha_{k}\in\mathcal{H}_{\eta}}\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon\leq 0
, we multiply the first equation of
\widetilde{\theta}_{n}
by
\epsilon
and plug in the expansion of
P_{\mathcal{H}_{\eta}}\eta
to get

\displaystyle-\sum_{k:\alpha_{k}\in\mathcal{H}_{\eta},\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon<0}\exp(l_{n}\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon_{n})\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon+n\sum_{j=1}^{m}\gamma_{k_{j}}(\alpha_{k_{j}}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon)=l_{n}\epsilon_{n}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon.

Since
-\sum_{k:\alpha_{k}\in\mathcal{H}_{\eta},\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon<0}\exp(l_{n}\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon_{n})\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon\rightarrow 0
,
n\sum_{j=1}^{m}\gamma_{k_{j}}(\alpha_{k_{j}}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon)\leq 0
and
l_{n}\epsilon_{n}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon\rightarrow\infty
, this leads to contradiction. So we have
\max_{k:\alpha_{k}\in\mathcal{H}_{\eta}}\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon>0
, then follow similar proof as in part (1), we have
\widetilde{\theta}_{n}=O(\log n)
. So we have
\theta_{n}=o(n)+nP_{\mathcal{H}_{\eta}^{\perp}}\eta
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\theta_{n}}{n}=P_{\mathcal{H}_{\eta}^{\perp}}\eta.

Since
\theta_{n}
is unique, this implies that
P_{\mathcal{H}_{\eta}^{\perp}}\eta
is uniquely determined.

Continuity:
For any sequence
\left\{\eta_{n}\right\}
converging to
\eta
, i.e.,
\eta_{n}\rightarrow\eta
, the problem falls into two cases:

Case 1:
If the choice of
\mathcal{H}_{\eta}
is proper (satisfies the condition in part (1)) in a neighborhood of
\eta
, then the continuity of
P_{\mathcal{H}_{\eta}^{\perp}}\eta
follows by the continuity of regular projection.

Case 2:
If the choice of
\mathcal{H}_{\eta}
is not proper in any neighborhood of
\eta
, this implies that there exists
k_{j}\in\left\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{m}\right\}
such that
\gamma_{k_{p}}=0
in the expansion of
P_{\mathcal{H}_{\eta}}\eta
due to the continuity of projection. We assume WLOG that
\gamma_{k_{j}}>0
for
j=1,\ldots,p-1,p+1,\ldots,m
. Denote
\mathcal{H}=span\left\{\alpha_{k_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{k_{p-1}},\alpha_{k_{p+1}},\ldots,\alpha_{k_{m}}\right\}
. Since
P_{\mathcal{H}_{\eta}}\eta=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\gamma_{k_{j}}\alpha_{k_{j}}

, there exists
\gamma_{j,n}\rightarrow\gamma_{k_{j}}
for
j=1,\ldots,m
such that

\displaystyle P_{\mathcal{H}_{\eta}}\eta_{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\gamma_{j,n}\alpha_{k_{j}}.

Since
\gamma_{p,n}\rightarrow\gamma_{k_{p}}=0
and
\sum_{j\neq p}\gamma_{j,n}\alpha_{k_{j}}\in\mathcal{H}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|P_{\mathcal{H}_{\eta}}\eta_{n}-P_{\mathcal{H}}\eta_{n}\right\|=\left\|P_{\mathcal{H}_{\eta}}\eta_{n}-P_{\mathcal{H}}\left(P_{\mathcal{H}_{\eta}}\eta_{n}\right)\right\|\leq\left\|P_{\mathcal{H}_{\eta}}\eta_{n}-\sum_{j\neq p}\gamma_{j,n}\alpha_{k_{j}}\right\|=\left\|\gamma_{p,n}\alpha_{k_{p}}\right\|\rightarrow 0.

By continuity of projection, this implies that either
\mathcal{H}_{\eta}
or
\mathcal{H}
is proper for
\left\{\eta_{n}\right\}
. Since by continuity of projection, we have
\left\|P_{\mathcal{H}_{\eta}}\eta_{n}-P_{\mathcal{H}_{\eta}}\eta\right\|\rightarrow 0
. So we have
\left\|P_{\mathcal{H}}\eta_{n}-P_{\mathcal{H}_{\eta}}\eta\right\|\rightarrow 0
and
\left\|P_{\mathcal{H}_{\eta}}\eta_{n}-P_{\mathcal{H}_{\eta}}\eta\right\|\rightarrow 0
, which implies that
P_{\mathcal{H}_{\eta_{n}}}\eta_{n}
converges to
P_{\mathcal{H}_{\eta}}\eta
. So
P_{\mathcal{H}_{\eta}^{\perp}}\eta
is continuous with respect to
\eta
.
∎

S.2.7
Proof of Proposition
5
and Corollary
2

Proof of Proposition
5
.

Define
\Omega=\{(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J}):\|P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{\eta(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J})}}\eta(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J})\|=\max_{\eta\in\mathcal{G}}\|P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{\eta}}\eta\|\}
. We then prove that
\Omega
has only one element
(1,0,\ldots,0)
. By the continuity of canonical projection and compactness of
\mathcal{E}\triangleq\{(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J}):0\leq\nu_{j}\leq 1,\sum_{j=1}^{J}\nu_{j}=1\}
,
\Omega
is non-empty. Then for any
j=2,\ldots,J
, denote
M_{j}=\sup\left\{\nu_{j}:\exists(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J})\in\Omega\right\}
. Since
\mathcal{E}
is a compact set, we can find
(\widetilde{\nu}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{\nu}_{J})\in\Omega
such that
\widetilde{\nu}_{j}=M_{j}
. So there holds
M_{j}<1
since
\|P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{\eta_{j}}}\eta_{j}\|<\|P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{\eta_{1}}}\eta_{1}\|
by assumption.

If
0<M_{j}<1
, then there exists
i\neq j
such that
\widetilde{\nu}_{i}>0
. We denote
\eta(\delta)=\eta(\widetilde{\nu}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{\nu}_{J})+\delta(\eta_{i}-\eta_{j})
, where
\eta(\delta)
falls into proper domain for
|\delta|
small enough. By the definition of
\Omega
,
\|P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{\eta({\delta})}}\eta({\delta})\|\leq\|P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{\eta(0)}}\eta(0)\|
. By the result in (1), there exists
\left\{\alpha_{k_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{k_{m}}\right\}\subseteq\left\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{K}\right\}
such that
\mathcal{H}_{\eta(0)}=span\left\{\alpha_{k_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{k_{m}}\right\}
and
P_{\mathcal{H}_{\eta(0)}}\eta(0)=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\gamma_{k_{j}}\alpha_{k_{j}}
, where
\gamma_{k_{1}},\ldots,\gamma_{k_{m}}
are nonnegative constants. Then the problem falls into either of the two cases:

Case 1
: The choice of
\mathcal{H}_{\eta({0})}
is proper for
\eta({\delta})
when
\delta
is in a neighborhood of 0, then

\displaystyle\left\|P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{\eta({\delta})}}\eta({\delta})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{\eta({0})}}\eta({\delta})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{\eta(0)}}\eta(0)\right\|^{2}+2\delta\left(P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{\eta(0)}}\eta(0)\right)^{\mathrm{T}}\left(P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{\eta(0)}}(\eta_{i}-\eta_{j})\right)+\delta^{2}\left\|P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{\eta(0)}}(\eta_{i}-\eta_{j})\right\|^{2}.

(S.131)

Since
\|P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{\eta({\delta})}}\eta({\delta})\|
attains maximum value at
\delta=0
, (
S.2.7
) implies that

\displaystyle P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{\eta(0)}}(\eta_{i}-\eta_{j})=0.

Then (
S.2.7
) indicates that
\|P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{\eta({\delta})}}\eta({\delta})\|=\|P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{\eta(0)}}\eta(0)\|
when
\delta
is in a small neighborhood of 0.

Case 2
: The choice of
\mathcal{H}_{\eta({0})}
is not proper for
\eta({\delta})
in any neighborhood of 0. Then by similar proof as in Proposition
4
, we assume WLOG that there exists
p=1,\ldots,m
such that
\gamma_{k_{p}}=0
and
\gamma_{k_{j}}>0
for
j=1,\ldots,p-1,p+1,\ldots,m
. Denote

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}\triangleq span\left\{\alpha_{k_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{k_{p-1}},\alpha_{k_{p+1}},\ldots,\alpha_{k_{m}}\right\}\subsetneq\mathcal{H}_{\eta(0)}\triangleq\mathcal{H}.

By Lemma
2
, we assume WLOG that
\left\{\alpha_{k_{j}}:j=1,\ldots,m,j\neq p\right\}
are linearly independent. Similar to the proof in Proposition
4
, either
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}
or
\mathcal{H}
is proper in a small neighborhood of 0. In the following proof, we simplified
\alpha_{k_{p}}
as
\alpha
. Denote matrix
\widetilde{Q}=(\alpha_{k_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{k_{p-1}},\alpha_{k_{p+1}},\ldots,\alpha_{k_{m}})
,
{Q}=(\alpha_{k_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{k_{p-1}},\alpha_{k_{p+1}},\ldots,\alpha_{k_{m}},\alpha)
,
\widetilde{H}=\widetilde{Q}\left(\widetilde{Q}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{Q}\right)^{-1}\widetilde{Q}^{\mathrm{T}}
and
H=Q\left(Q^{\mathrm{T}}Q\right)^{-1}Q^{\mathrm{T}}
. Since
\gamma_{k_{p}}=0
, we have
\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}P_{\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}\eta(0)=0
. Then we have
\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}(I-\widetilde{H})\eta(0)=0
.

If
\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}P_{\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}(\eta_{i}-\eta_{j})=0
, then

\displaystyle\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}P_{\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}\eta(\delta)=\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}P_{\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}\eta(0)+\delta\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}P_{\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}(\eta_{i}-\eta_{j})=0,

which implies that the choice of
\mathcal{H}
is proper for
\eta({\delta})
when
\delta
is in a neighborhood of 0, which indicates that the problem should fall into Case 1.

If
\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}P_{\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}(\eta_{i}-\eta_{j})\neq 0
, we assume WLOG that
\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}P_{\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}(\eta_{i}-\eta_{j})>0
. Then for
\delta\geq 0
,
\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}P_{\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}\eta(\delta)=\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}P_{\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}\eta(0)+\delta\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}P_{\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}(\eta_{i}-\eta_{j})\geq 0
. This implies that for small enough
\delta\geq 0
,
\mathcal{H}_{\eta(\delta)}
can be chosen as
\mathcal{H}
. Then for
\delta\geq 0
small enough,

\displaystyle\|P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{\eta({\delta})}}\eta({\delta})\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}}\eta({\delta})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}}\eta({0})\right\|^{2}+2\delta\left(P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}}\eta(0)\right)^{\mathrm{T}}\left(P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}}(\eta_{i}-\eta_{j})\right)+\left\|P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}}(\eta_{i}-\eta_{j})\right\|^{2}.

(S.132)

Since
\|P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{\eta({\delta})}}\eta({\delta})\|\leq\|P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{\eta({0})}}\eta({0})\|=\left\|P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}}\eta({0})\right\|
, (
S.2.7
) implies that

\displaystyle\left(P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}}\eta(0)\right)^{\mathrm{T}}\left(P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}}(\eta_{i}-\eta_{j})\right)\leq 0.

(S.133)

On the other side, for
\delta<0
,
\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}P_{\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}\eta(\delta)=\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}P_{\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}\eta(0)+\delta\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}P_{\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}(\eta_{i}-\eta_{j})<0
. This implies that for
\delta<0
enough close to 0,
\mathcal{H}_{\eta(\delta)}
can be chosen as
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}
. Since
\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}(I-\widetilde{H})\eta(0)=0
, we have

\displaystyle~~~~\|P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{\eta({\delta})}}\eta({\delta})\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|P_{\mathcal{\widetilde{H}}^{\perp}}\eta({\delta})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left[\eta(0)+\delta(\eta_{i}-\eta_{j})\right]^{\mathrm{T}}(I-\widetilde{H})\left[\eta(0)+\delta(\eta_{i}-\eta_{j})\right]

\displaystyle\geq\eta^{\mathrm{T}}(0)(I-\widetilde{H})\eta(0)+2\delta((I-\widetilde{H})\eta(0))^{\mathrm{T}}(\eta_{i}-\eta_{j})

\displaystyle=\eta^{\mathrm{T}}(0)\left(I-H+\frac{(I-\widetilde{H})\alpha\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}(I-\widetilde{H})}{\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}(I-\widetilde{H})\alpha}\right)\eta(0)+2\delta\eta^{\mathrm{T}}(0)\left(I-H+\frac{(I-\widetilde{H})\alpha\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}(I-\widetilde{H})}{\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}(I-\widetilde{H})\alpha}\right)(\eta_{i}-\eta_{j})

\displaystyle=\eta^{\mathrm{T}}(0)\left(I-H\right)\eta(0)+2\delta\eta^{\mathrm{T}}(0)\left(I-H\right)(\eta_{i}-\eta_{j})

\displaystyle=\left\|P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}}\eta(0)\right\|^{2}+2\delta\left(P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}}\eta({\delta})\right)^{\mathrm{T}}\left(P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}}(\eta_{i}-\eta_{j})\right).

(S.134)

The third last step of (
S.2.7
) is due to the following calculation:

\displaystyle{H}=\widetilde{H}+\frac{(I-\widetilde{H})\alpha\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}(I-\widetilde{H})}{\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}(I-\widetilde{H})\alpha}.

Since
\|P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{\eta({\delta})}}\eta({\delta})\|\leq\|P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{\eta({0})}}\eta({0})\|=\left\|P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}}\eta({0})\right\|
, (
S.2.7
) implies that

\displaystyle\left(P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}}\eta(0)\right)^{\mathrm{T}}\left(P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}}(\eta_{i}-\eta_{j})\right)\geq 0.

(S.135)

Combine (
S.133
) and (
S.135
), we have

\displaystyle(P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{\eta(0)}}\eta(0))^{\mathrm{T}}(P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{\eta(0)}}(\eta_{i}-\eta_{j}))=(P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}}\eta(0))^{\mathrm{T}}(P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}}(\eta_{i}-\eta_{j}))=0.

Then by (
S.2.7
) and (
S.2.7
), there holds
\|P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{\eta({\delta})}}\eta({\delta})\|=\|P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{\eta({0})}}\eta({0})\|
for any
\delta
in a small neighborhood of 0. This implies that for
\delta>0
small enough, we have
(\bar{\nu}_{1},\ldots,\bar{\nu}_{i}-\delta,\ldots,\bar{\nu}_{j}+\delta,\ldots,\bar{\nu}_{J})\in\Omega
, which contradicts with the definition of
M_{j}
. So
M_{j}=0
for
j=2,\ldots,J
, which indicates that the element in
\Omega
can only be
(1,0,\ldots,0)
since
\Omega
is nonempty. Hence
\eta_{1}
is the unique maximizer in
\mathcal{G}
.
∎

From the proof of Proposition
5
, we can easily prove Corollary
2
.

Proof of Corollary
2
.

Following the proof in Proposition
5
, since
(1,0,\ldots,0)
is the maximizer, by the arguments in (
S.2.7
) and (
S.2.7
), we have
(P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{\eta_{1}}}\eta_{1}-P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{\eta_{1}}}\eta_{j})^{\mathrm{T}}P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{\eta_{1}}}\eta_{1}>0
for any
j=2,\ldots,J
, or
(1,0,\ldots,0)
will not be the only element in
\Omega
. Then we have:

\displaystyle(\eta_{1}-\eta_{j})^{\mathrm{T}}P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{\eta_{1}}}\eta_{1}=(P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{\eta_{1}}}\eta_{1}-P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{\eta_{1}}}\eta_{j})^{\mathrm{T}}P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{\eta_{1}}}\eta_{1}>0.

∎

S.2.8
Proof of Proposition
6

Proof of Proposition
6
.

Define

\displaystyle f_{n}(\theta)=-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\exp\left(\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta\right)+n\eta^{\mathrm{T}}\theta-\frac{1}{2}\theta^{\mathrm{T}}\theta

and denote the unique maximum point of
f_{n}
by
\theta_{n}
. Furthermore, suppose
l_{n}=\left\|\theta_{n}\right\|
,
\epsilon_{n}=\theta_{n}/l_{n}\rightarrow\epsilon
. Following the proof in Proposition
4
, we have
\underset{k=1,\ldots,K}{\max}\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon>0
. Then by the proof in Proposition
8
, there exists
\left\{\alpha_{k_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{k_{m}}\right\}\subseteq\left\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{K}\right\}
and positive constants
\gamma_{k_{1}},\ldots,\gamma_{k_{m}}>0
such that
\eta=\sum_{p=1}^{m}\gamma_{k_{p}}\alpha_{k_{p}}
. Furthermore, there holds
\alpha_{k_{1}}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon=\ldots=\alpha_{k_{m}}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon=\underset{k=1,\ldots,K}{\max}\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon
. So the existence of canonical expansion is proved.

If there exists two canonical expansion:

\displaystyle\eta=\sum_{p=1}^{m_{1}}\gamma_{k_{p}}\alpha_{k_{p}}=\sum_{p=1}^{m_{2}}\widetilde{\gamma}_{l_{p}}\alpha_{l_{p}},

where
\gamma_{k_{1}},\ldots,\gamma_{k_{m_{1}}},\widetilde{\gamma}_{l_{1}},\ldots,\widetilde{\gamma}_{l_{m_{2}}}>0
with
\epsilon
and
\widetilde{\epsilon}
satisfying the condition. Then we have

\displaystyle\eta^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=1}^{m_{1}}\gamma_{k_{p}}\alpha_{k_{p}}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon=\Big(\sum_{p=1}^{m_{1}}\gamma_{k_{p}}\Big)\underset{k=1,\ldots,K}{\max}\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon,

\displaystyle\eta^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=1}^{m_{2}}\widetilde{\gamma}_{l_{p}}\alpha_{l_{p}}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon\leq\big(\sum_{p=1}^{m_{2}}\widetilde{\gamma}_{l_{p}}\big)\underset{k=1,\ldots,K}{\max}\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon,

which implies that
\sum_{p=1}^{m_{1}}\gamma_{k_{p}}\leq\sum_{p=1}^{m_{2}}\widetilde{\gamma}_{l_{p}}
. Similarly we have

\displaystyle\eta^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\epsilon}

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=1}^{m_{1}}\gamma_{k_{p}}\alpha_{k_{p}}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\epsilon}\leq\Big(\sum_{p=1}^{m_{1}}\gamma_{k_{p}}\Big)\underset{k=1,\ldots,K}{\max}\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\epsilon},

\displaystyle\eta^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\epsilon}

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=1}^{m_{2}}\widetilde{\gamma}_{l_{p}}\alpha_{l_{p}}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\epsilon}=\Big(\sum_{p=1}^{m_{2}}\widetilde{\gamma}_{l_{p}}\Big)\underset{k=1,\ldots,K}{\max}\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\epsilon},

which implies that
\sum_{p=1}^{m_{1}}\gamma_{k_{p}}\geq\sum_{p=1}^{m_{2}}\widetilde{\gamma}_{l_{p}}
. So we have
\sum_{p=1}^{m_{1}}\gamma_{k_{p}}=\sum_{p=1}^{m_{2}}\widetilde{\gamma}_{l_{p}}
.
∎

S.2.9
Proof of Proposition
7

Proof of Proposition
7
.

By the given conditions,
\widehat{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the unique solution of the linear equation:

\displaystyle\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta=\begin{cases}\nu_{j}&\text{ if }\alpha_{k}\in U_{j}\\
0&\text{ if }\alpha_{k}\in V_{0}\end{cases}

with the constraint:
\theta-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\widehat{c}_{j}\varphi_{j}\in\mathcal{H}
. By continuity, for any
c
in a small neighborhood of
\widehat{c}
, the unique solution to the above linear equation with constraint:
\theta-\sum_{j=1}^{m}{c}_{j}\varphi_{j}\in\mathcal{H}
still satisfies Conditions (i)-(iv) in Proposition
7
. We denote this unique solution by
\theta_{c}
. Then for any
{\bm{\zeta}}^{(n,c)}=(\zeta_{1}^{(n,c)},\ldots,\zeta_{m}^{(n,c)})
such that
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}(\zeta_{1}^{(n,c)},\ldots,\zeta_{m}^{(n,c)})/\log n=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{m})
, let
\widetilde{\theta}_{n,c}
be the unique solution of the following linear equation satisfying:

(i)

\widetilde{\theta}_{n,c}-\log n\sum_{j=1}^{m}{c}_{j}\varphi_{j}\in\mathcal{H}
.

(ii)

For
j=1,\ldots,J
and any
\alpha_{k}\in U_{j}
, there holds
\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\theta}_{n,c}=\log(\gamma_{k}\xi_{j}^{(n)}-\beta_{k}\log n)-\log\omega_{k}
, where
\beta_{k}
is the coefficient of
\alpha_{k}
in the expansion of
\xi-\sum_{j=1}^{m}{c}_{j}\varphi_{j}
under basis
U_{1}\cup\ldots\cup U_{J}\cup V_{0}
.

(iii)

For any
\alpha_{k}\in V_{0}
, there holds
\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\theta}_{n,c}=\log\left(-\beta_{k}\log n\right)
, where
\beta_{k}<0
is the coefficient of
\alpha_{k}
in the expansion of
\theta_{c}-\sum_{j=1}^{m}{c}_{j}\varphi_{j}
under basis
U_{1}\cup\ldots\cup U_{J}\cup V_{0}
.

We can easily prove that
\widetilde{\theta}_{n,c}/\log n\rightarrow\theta_{c}
. Then we plug
\widetilde{\theta}_{n,c}
into the gradient of
f_{n}(\cdot|\bm{\xi}^{(n)},{\bm{\zeta}}^{(n,c)})
:

\displaystyle\nabla f_{n}(\widetilde{\theta}_{n,c}|\bm{\xi}^{(n)},{\bm{\zeta}}^{(n,c)})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{\alpha_{k}\in V_{-}}\omega_{k}\exp(\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\theta}_{n,c})\alpha_{k}-\sum_{\alpha_{k}\in V_{0}}\omega_{k}\exp(\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\theta}_{n,c})\alpha_{k}-\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{\alpha_{k}\in U_{j}}\omega_{k}\exp(\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\theta}_{n,c})\alpha_{k}

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{\alpha_{k}\in U_{j}}\gamma_{k}\xi_{j}^{(n)}\alpha_{k}+\sum_{j=1}^{m}{\zeta}_{j}^{(n,c)}\varphi_{j}-\widetilde{\theta}_{n,c}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle o(\log n)+\log n\big[\sum_{\alpha_{k}\in V^{0}}\beta_{k}\alpha_{k}+\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{\alpha_{k}\in U_{j}}\beta_{k}\alpha_{k}+\sum_{j=1}^{m}{c}_{j}\varphi_{j}\big]-\widetilde{\theta}_{n,c}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle o(\log n)+\log n~\theta_{c}-\widetilde{\theta}_{n,c}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle o(\log n).

(S.136)

Similar to the proof in Lemma
3
, we have
\|\theta_{n}(\bm{\xi}^{(n)},{\bm{\zeta}}^{(n,c)})-\widetilde{\theta}_{n,c}\|=o(\log n)
. Then by (
S.2.9
) we have

\displaystyle 0\leq

\displaystyle f_{n}({\theta}_{n}(\bm{\xi}^{(n)},{\bm{\zeta}}^{(n,c)})|\bm{\xi}^{(n)},{\bm{\zeta}}^{(n,c)})-f_{n}(\widetilde{\theta}_{n,c}|\bm{\xi}^{(n)},{\bm{\zeta}}^{(n,c)})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(\nabla f_{n}(\widetilde{\theta}_{n,c}|\bm{\xi}^{(n)},{\bm{\zeta}}^{(n,c)}))^{\mathrm{T}}({\theta}_{n}(\bm{\xi}^{(n)},{\bm{\zeta}}^{(n,c)})-\widetilde{\theta}_{n,c})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle o(\log^{2}n).

(S.137)

We assume WLOG that
U_{1}=\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{p_{1}}\},\ldots,U_{J}=\{\alpha_{p_{J-1}+1},\ldots,\alpha_{p_{J}}\}
and
V_{0}=\{\alpha_{p_{J}+1},\ldots,\alpha_{p}\}
. Denote
X=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{p})
and denote
\beta=\big(\nu_{1}\cdot\mathbf{1}^{\mathrm{T}}_{p_{1}},\nu_{2}\cdot\mathbf{1}^{\mathrm{T}}_{p_{2}-p_{1}},\ldots,\nu_{J}\cdot\mathbf{1}^{\mathrm{T}}_{p_{J}-p_{J-1}},0\cdot\mathbf{1}^{\mathrm{T}}_{p-p_{J}}\big)^{\mathrm{T}}
. Since
\theta_{c}-\sum_{j=1}^{m}{c}_{j}\varphi_{j}\in\mathcal{H}
, suppose that
\theta_{c}-\sum_{j=1}^{m}{c}_{j}\varphi_{j}=X\alpha
for
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, then the following linear equation holds:

\displaystyle\beta=X^{\mathrm{T}}\theta_{c}=X^{\mathrm{T}}(X\alpha+\sum_{j=1}^{m}{c}_{j}\varphi_{j}),

which implies that
\alpha=(X^{\mathrm{T}}X)^{-1}\left(\beta-\sum_{j=1}^{m}{c}_{j}X^{\mathrm{T}}\varphi_{j}\right)
. So we have
\theta_{c}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}{c}_{j}\varphi_{j}+X(X^{\mathrm{T}}X)^{-1}\\
\left(\beta-\sum_{j=1}^{m}{c}_{j}X^{\mathrm{T}}\varphi_{j}\right)\triangleq\widetilde{\theta}+\sum_{j=1}^{m}{c}_{j}P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}}\varphi_{j}
. Here
\widetilde{\theta}=X(X^{\mathrm{T}}X)^{-1}\beta
does not depend on
c
. Then we have

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}{c}_{j}\varphi_{j}\right)^{\mathrm{T}}\xi_{c}-\frac{1}{2}\xi_{c}^{\mathrm{T}}\xi_{c}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}{c}_{j}\varphi_{j}+X(X^{\mathrm{T}}X)^{-1}\left(\beta-\sum_{j=1}^{m}{c}_{j}X^{\mathrm{T}}\varphi_{j}\right)\right)\left(-\sum_{j=1}^{m}{c}_{j}\varphi_{j}+X(X^{\mathrm{T}}X)^{-1}\left(\beta-\sum_{j=1}^{m}{c}_{j}X^{\mathrm{T}}\varphi_{j}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left\|\sum_{j=1}^{m}{c}_{j}P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}}\varphi_{j}\right\|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\beta^{\mathrm{T}}(X^{\mathrm{T}}X)^{-1}\beta+\beta^{\mathrm{T}}(X^{\mathrm{T}}X)^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{m}{c}_{j}X^{\mathrm{T}}\varphi_{j}.

(S.138)

Hence by the definition of
\widetilde{\theta}_{n,c}
, (
S.2.9
) and (
S.2.9
), we have

\displaystyle f_{n}(\theta_{n}(\bm{\xi}^{(n)},{\bm{\zeta}}^{(n,c)})|\bm{\xi}^{(n)},{\bm{\zeta}}^{(n,c)})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle o(\log^{2}n)-\sum_{\alpha_{k}\in V_{-}}\omega_{k}\exp(\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\theta}_{n,c})-\sum_{\alpha_{k}\in V_{0}}\omega_{k}\exp(\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\theta}_{n,c})-\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{\alpha_{k}\in U_{j}}\omega_{k}\exp(\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\theta}_{n,c})

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{J}\xi_{j}^{(n)}\sum_{\alpha_{k}\in U_{j}}\gamma_{k}\big[\log(\gamma_{k}\xi_{j}^{(n)}-\beta_{k}\log n)-\log\omega_{k}\big]+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\zeta_{j}^{(n,c)}\varphi_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\theta}_{n,c}-\frac{1}{2}\widetilde{\theta}_{n,c}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\theta}_{n,c}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle o(\log^{2}n)+\sum_{j=1}^{J}\xi_{j}^{(n)}\sum_{\alpha_{k}\in U_{j}}\big(-\gamma_{k}+\gamma_{k}\log(\gamma_{k}\xi_{j}^{(n)})-\log\omega_{k}\big)+\log^{2}n\left(\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}{c}_{j}\varphi_{j}\right)^{\mathrm{T}}\xi_{c}-\frac{1}{2}\xi_{c}^{\mathrm{T}}\xi_{c}\right)

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle D_{n,1}+\log^{2}n\left(c^{\mathrm{T}}D_{2}+\frac{1}{2}\left\|\sum_{j=1}^{m}{c}_{j}P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}}\varphi_{j}\right\|^{2}+o(1)\right),

where
D_{n,1},D_{2}
does not depend on
c
.
∎

S.2.10
Proof of Proposition
8

To verify Proposition
8
, we first prove the following three lemmas.

Lemma 2
.

Let
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{K}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be
d
-vectors and
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{K}
be nonnegative constants. Let
\xi=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\gamma_{k}\alpha_{k}
. Then there exists
\{\alpha_{k_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{k_{m}}\}\subseteq\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{K}\}
such that
\xi=\sum_{p=1}^{m}\widetilde{\gamma}_{k_{p}}\alpha_{k_{p}}
, where
\widetilde{\gamma}_{k_{1}},\ldots,\widetilde{\gamma}_{k_{m}}
are positive constants and
\alpha_{k_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{k_{m}}
are linearly independent.

Lemma 3
.

Let
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{K}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
be distinct
d
-vectors ad let
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{M}>0
be positive constants. Then the vector
\widehat{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
that satisfies the following condition is unique if exists:

(i)

For
k=1,\ldots,M
, there holds
\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}=\gamma_{k}
.

(ii)

There exists
\{\alpha_{j_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{j_{p}}\}\subseteq\{\alpha_{M+1},\ldots,\alpha_{K}\}
such that

(a)

\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{M},\alpha_{j_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{j_{p}}\}
are linearly independent.

(b)

\widehat{\theta}\in span\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{M},\alpha_{j_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{j_{p}}\}
. For any
m=1,\ldots,p
, the coefficient of
\alpha_{j_{m}}
in the expansion of
\widehat{\theta}
under basis
\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{M},\alpha_{j_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{j_{p}}\}
is negative.

(c)

For
\alpha\in\{\alpha_{j_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{j_{p}}\}
, there holds
\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}=0
.

(d)

For
\alpha\in\{\alpha_{M+1},\ldots,\alpha_{K}\}\setminus\{\alpha_{j_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{j_{p}}\}
, there holds
\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}<0
.

Lemma 4
.

Let
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{K},\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{J}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
be
d
-vectors,
\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{K}
and
\widetilde{\nu}_{1}>\ldots>\widetilde{\nu}_{J}>0
be positive constants. Suppose
\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{J}\in X\triangleq\{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\gamma_{k}\alpha_{k}:\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{K}\geq 0\}
. Then we can define continuous
\theta(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J})
in a neighborhood
\mathcal{O}
of
(\widetilde{\nu}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{\nu}_{J})
such that for any
(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J})\in\mathcal{O}
, any
(\xi_{1}^{(n)},\ldots,\xi_{J}^{(n)})
satisfying

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{(\log\xi_{1}^{(n)},\ldots,\log\xi_{J}^{(n)})}{\log n}=(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J}),

the unique maximizer
\theta_{n}
of the following function:

\displaystyle f_{n}(\theta)=-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\exp(\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta)+\left(\sum_{j=1}^{J}\xi_{j}^{(n)}\eta_{j}\right)^{\mathrm{T}}\theta-\frac{1}{2}\theta^{\mathrm{T}}\theta

satisfies the following convergence result:

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\theta_{n}}{\log n}=\theta(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J}).

Proof of Lemma
2
.

We assume WLOG that
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{K}>0
. If
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{K}
are linearly independent, then the result is proved. If not, then there exists
1\leq k_{1}<\ldots<k_{m}\leq K
and nonzero constants
b_{k_{1}},\ldots,b_{k_{m}}
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{p=1}^{m}b_{k_{p}}\alpha_{k_{p}}=0.

We assume WLOG that
\gamma_{k_{1}}/{b_{k_{1}}}\leq\ldots\leq\gamma_{k_{m}}/{b_{k_{m}}}
and divide the problem into two cases:

Case 1:
If
0<\gamma_{k_{1}}/{b_{k_{1}}}\leq\ldots\leq\gamma_{k_{m}}/{b_{k_{m}}}
, then
b_{k_{1}},\ldots,b_{k_{m}}>0
. We expand
\alpha_{k_{1}}
in terms of
\alpha_{k_{2}},\ldots,\alpha_{k_{m}}
and obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{p=1}^{m}{\gamma}_{k_{p}}\alpha_{k_{p}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=2}^{m}{\gamma}_{k_{p}}\alpha_{k_{p}}-\frac{\gamma_{k_{1}}}{b_{k_{1}}}\big(\sum_{p=2}^{m}b_{k_{p}}\alpha_{k_{p}}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=2}^{m}b_{k_{p}}\big(\frac{\gamma_{k_{p}}}{b_{k_{p}}}-\frac{\gamma_{k_{1}}}{b_{k_{1}}}\big)\alpha_{k_{p}},

where
b_{k_{p}}(\frac{\gamma_{k_{p}}}{b_{k_{p}}}-\frac{\gamma_{k_{1}}}{b_{k_{1}}})>0
for
p=2,\ldots,m
.

Case 2:
If there exists
1\leq q\leq m
such that
\gamma_{k_{q}}/b_{k_{q}}<0<\gamma_{k_{q+1}}/b_{k_{q+1}}
(If all terms are negative, then
q=m
). We expand
\alpha_{k_{q}}
in terms of
\alpha_{k_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{k_{q-1}},\alpha_{k_{q+1}},\ldots,\alpha_{k_{m}}
and obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{p=1}^{m}{\gamma}_{k_{p}}\alpha_{k_{p}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{p\neq q}{\gamma}_{k_{p}}\alpha_{k_{p}}-\frac{\gamma_{k_{q}}}{b_{k_{q}}}\big(\sum_{p\neq q}b_{k_{p}}\alpha_{k_{p}}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{p\neq q}\gamma_{k_{p}}\frac{(\frac{\gamma_{k_{p}}}{b_{k_{p}}})-(\frac{\gamma_{k_{q}}}{b_{k_{q}}})}{(\frac{\gamma_{k_{p}}}{b_{k_{p}}})}\alpha_{k_{p}}.

It is easy to see that for any
p\neq q
, there holds
[(\frac{\gamma_{k_{p}}}{b_{k_{p}}})-(\frac{\gamma_{k_{q}}}{b_{k_{q}}})]/(\frac{\gamma_{k_{p}}}{b_{k_{p}}})>0
. So in either case, we can expand
\xi
by at most
K-1
vectors chosen from
\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{K}\}
with positive coefficients. This implies that we can continue procedure and it will end over finite steps. Then the final remaining vectors and coefficients satisfy the condition.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3
.

Define

\displaystyle f_{n}(\theta)=-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\exp(\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta)+\big(\sum_{k=1}^{M}n^{\gamma_{k}}\alpha_{k}\big)^{\mathrm{T}}\theta-\frac{1}{2}\theta^{\mathrm{T}}\theta

and denote the unique maximizer of
f_{n}
by
\theta_{n}
. By similar method as in Proposition
4
, we can prove that
\theta_{n}=O(\log n)
.

If
\widehat{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfies all the conditions, then we let
\widetilde{\theta}_{n}
be the unique solution of the following equation:

(i)

\widetilde{\theta}_{n}\in span\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{M},\alpha_{j_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{j_{p}}\}
.

(ii)

For
k=1,\ldots,M
, let
\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\theta}_{n}=\log(n^{\gamma_{k}}-\beta_{k}\log n)=\log(n^{\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}}-\beta_{k}\log n)
, where
\beta_{k}
is the coefficient of
\alpha_{k}
in the expansion of
\widehat{\theta}
under basis
\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{M},\alpha_{j_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{j_{p}}\}
.

(iii)

For
m=1,\ldots,p
, let
\alpha_{j_{m}}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\theta}_{n}=\log(-\zeta_{m}\log n)
, where
\zeta_{m}
is the coefficient of
\alpha_{j_{m}}
in the expansion of
\widehat{\theta}
under basis
\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{M},\alpha_{j_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{j_{p}}\}
.

Then it is easy to prove that
\widetilde{\theta}_{n}/\log n\rightarrow\widehat{\theta}
. Now we plug
\widetilde{\theta}_{n}
into the gradient of
f_{n}
:

\displaystyle\nabla f_{n}(\widetilde{\theta}_{n})=

\displaystyle-\sum_{k=1}^{M}(n^{\gamma_{k}}-\beta_{k}\log n)\alpha_{k}+\sum_{m=1}^{p}\zeta_{m}\log n+\sum_{k=1}^{M}n^{\gamma_{k}}\alpha_{k}

\displaystyle~~~~~~-\log n\big[\sum_{k=1}^{M}\beta_{k}\alpha_{k}+\sum_{m=1}^{p}\zeta_{m}\big]-(\widetilde{\theta}_{n}-\log n~\widehat{\theta})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(\widetilde{\theta}_{n}-\log n~\widehat{\theta})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle o(\log n).

(S.139)

By Taylor expansion we have

\displaystyle 0=\nabla f_{n}({\theta}_{n})=\nabla f_{n}(\widetilde{\theta}_{n})+\nabla^{2}f_{n}(\theta_{n}^{\ast})({\theta}_{n}-\widetilde{\theta}_{n}).

(S.140)

Since
-\nabla^{2}f_{n}(\theta_{n}^{\ast})\succeq I_{d}
, (
S.2.10
) and (
S.140
) indicates that

\displaystyle\|{\theta}_{n}-\widetilde{\theta}_{n}\|=\|(\nabla^{2}f_{n}(\theta_{n}^{\ast}))^{-1}\nabla f_{n}(\widetilde{\theta}_{n})\|\leq\|\nabla f_{n}(\widetilde{\theta}_{n})\|=o(\log n).

(S.141)

Since we have
\widetilde{\theta}_{n}/\log n\rightarrow\widehat{\theta}
, (
S.141
) implies that
\theta_{n}/\log n\rightarrow\widehat{\theta}
. Since the maximizer
\theta_{n}
is unique,
\widehat{\theta}
that satisfies the conditions is also unique if exists.
∎

Proof of Lemma
4
.

We divide the proof into four steps. The sketch is as follows:

(1)

In Step 1, we prove that
\theta_{n}=O(\log n)
and define
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\theta_{n}/\log n\triangleq\theta(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J})
.

(2)

In Step 2, we introduce the concept of “characterization equation” and prove that we can construct a characterization equation at
(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J})
which has unique solution
\theta(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J})
.

(3)

In Step 3, we prove that the characterization equation at
(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J})
is unique.

(4)

In Step 4, we prove the lemma by the uniqueness of characterization equation.

Step 1:
Denote
l_{n}=\|\theta_{n}\|
and
\epsilon_{n}=\theta_{n}/l_{n}\rightarrow\epsilon
. Following the proof in Proposition
4
, there holds
l_{n}\rightarrow\infty
. We first prove that
{\max}_{k=1,\ldots,K}\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon>0
.

If this is not the case, we multiply both sides of the first order equation for
\theta_{n}
by
\epsilon
:

\displaystyle-\sum_{k:\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon<0}\exp(l_{n}\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon_{n})\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon+\left(\sum_{j=1}^{J}n^{\nu_{j}}\eta_{j}\right)^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon=l_{n}\epsilon_{n}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon.

(S.142)

Since
\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{J}\in X
and
{\max}_{k=1,\ldots,K}\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon\leq 0
, we have
\eta_{1}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon,\ldots,\eta_{J}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon\leq 0
. Since
0<\nu_{J}<\ldots<\nu_{1}
, we have
\left[\sum_{j=1}^{J}n^{\nu_{j}}\eta_{j}\right]^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon\leq 0
for
n
large enough. On the other side, we have
-\sum_{k:\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon<0}\omega_{k}\exp(l_{n}\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon_{n})\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon\rightarrow 0
and
l_{n}\epsilon_{n}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon\rightarrow\infty
, which contradicts with (
S.142
). So
\underset{k=1,\ldots,K}{\max}\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon\\
>0
. Then following the proof in Proposition
4
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{l_{n}}{\log n}=\frac{1}{{\max}_{k=1,\ldots,K}\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon}.

So we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\theta_{n}}{\log n}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{l_{n}}{\log n}\epsilon_{n}=\frac{1}{{\max}_{k=1,\ldots,K}\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon}\epsilon\triangleq\theta(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J}).

Step 2:
We first introduce the concept of characterization equation: For
k\in\left\{1,\ldots,d\right\}
, we call a set of
k
linear equations a characterization equation at
\left(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J}\right)
where
\nu_{1}>\ldots>\nu_{J}>0
if the
l
-th
(l=1,\ldots,k)
equation is of one of the following two types:

•

Type-1 equation:
\alpha_{j_{l}}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta=\xi_{l}
, where
\xi_{l}\in\left\{\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J},0\right\}
.

•

Type-2 equation:
\alpha_{j_{l}}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta=\zeta_{l}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta
, where
\zeta_{l}\in\left\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{K}\right\}\setminus\left\{\alpha_{j_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{j_{k}}\right\}
.

Here
\left\{\alpha_{j_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{j_{k}}\right\}\subseteq\left\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{K}\right\}
is a set of
k
vectors. Furthermore, the characterization equation is required to satisfy the following conditions:

(i)

\alpha_{j_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{j_{k}}
are linearly independent.

(ii)

The equation has unique solution
\widehat{\theta}
under constraint:
\theta\in span\left\{\alpha_{j_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{j_{k}}\right\}
.

(iii)

For
j=1,\ldots,J
, there exists unique
(\gamma_{j,1},\ldots,\gamma_{j,k})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
such that
\eta_{j}
has expansion:
\eta_{j}=\sum_{l=1}^{k}\gamma_{j,l}\alpha_{j_{l}}
. Suppose
\alpha_{l_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{l_{m}}
are all elements in
\left\{\alpha_{j_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{j_{k}}\right\}
such that
\alpha_{l_{1}}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}=\ldots=\alpha_{l_{m}}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}=\nu_{j}
, then there holds
\gamma_{j,l_{1}},\ldots,\gamma_{j,l_{m}}>0
. For any
l\in\left\{1,\ldots,k\right\}
such that
\alpha_{j_{l}}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}<\nu_{j}
, there holds
\gamma_{j,l}=0
.

(iv)

There exists unique
(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{k})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
such that
\widehat{\theta}
has expansion:
\widehat{\theta}=\sum_{l=1}^{k}\gamma_{l}\alpha_{j_{l}}
. Suppose
\alpha_{l_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{l_{m}}
are all elements in
\left\{\alpha_{j_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{j_{k}}\right\}
such that
\alpha_{l_{1}}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}=\ldots=\alpha_{l_{m}}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}=0
. Then there holds
\gamma_{l_{1}},\ldots,\gamma_{l_{m}}<0
.

(v)

For any
\alpha\in\left\{\alpha_{j_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{j_{k}}\right\}
, either
\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}\in\left\{\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J},0\right\}
or there exists unique
\beta\in\left\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{K}\right\}\setminus\left\{\alpha_{j_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{j_{k}}\right\}
such that
\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}=\beta^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}\notin\left\{\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J},0\right\}
and
0<\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}<\nu_{1}
. In the second case, there exists unique
\left(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{k}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
such that
\beta
has expansion:
\beta=\sum_{l=1}^{k}\gamma_{l}\alpha_{j_{l}}
. For any
l\in\left\{1,\ldots,k\right\}
such that
\alpha_{j_{l}}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}=\beta^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}
, there holds
\gamma_{l}<0
. For any
l\in\left\{1,\ldots,k\right\}
such that
\alpha_{j_{k}}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}<\beta^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}
, there holds
\gamma_{l}=0
.

(vi)

For any
\beta\in\left\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{K}\right\}\setminus\left\{\alpha_{j_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{j_{k}}\right\}
, either
\beta^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}<\min_{l=1,\ldots,k}\alpha_{j_{l}}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}
or there exists
\alpha\in\left\{\alpha_{j_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{j_{k}}\right\}
such that
\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}=\beta^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}\notin\left\{\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J},0\right\}
.

Then we construct a characterization equation at
(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J})
with unique solution
\theta(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J})
. For notation simplicity, we simplify
\theta(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J})
as
\widehat{\theta}
. We also assume that
(\xi_{1}^{(n)},\ldots,\xi_{J}^{(n)})=(n^{\nu_{1}},\ldots,n^{\nu_{J}})
in this step.

The first order equation for
\theta_{n}
is as

\displaystyle-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\exp(\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta_{n})\alpha_{k}+\sum_{j=1}^{J}n^{\nu_{j}}\eta_{j}=\theta_{n}.

(S.143)

For
j=1,\ldots,J
, denote
\mathcal{E}_{j}=\{\alpha_{k}\in\left\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{K}\right\}:\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}\geq\nu_{j}\}
. We construct set
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}_{j},j=1,\ldots,J
in the following inductive way:

Step 2.1:
For
j=1
, following the proof in Proposition
4
we have
\max_{k=1,\ldots,K}\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}=\nu_{1}
and there exists
\left\{\alpha_{k_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{k_{m}}\right\}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{1}
and positive constants
\gamma_{k_{1}},\ldots,\gamma_{k_{m}}>0
such that
\eta_{1}=\sum_{p=1}^{m}\gamma_{k_{p}}\alpha_{k_{p}}
.

We choose a maximal linearly independent subset of
\mathcal{E}_{1}
which contains
\left\{\alpha_{k_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{k_{m}}\right\}
to enter set
\mathcal{G}
. Then we have constructed
\mathcal{G}_{1}
with linearly independent components and
span\left(\mathcal{G}_{1}\right)=span\left(\mathcal{E}_{1}\right)\triangleq\mathcal{H}_{1}
. Furthermore,
\eta_{1}\in\mathcal{H}_{1}
.

Step 2.2:
If
\mathcal{E}_{i},\mathcal{G}_{i},\mathcal{H}_{i}
is constructed for
i=1,\ldots,j-1
and
\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{j-1}\in\mathcal{H}_{j-1}
, we project first order equation (
S.143
) on
\mathcal{H}_{j-1}
and divide both side by
n^{\nu_{j}}
to get

\displaystyle-\sum_{k:\alpha_{k}\in\mathcal{E}_{j}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}\frac{\exp(\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta_{n})}{n^{\nu_{j}}}P_{\mathcal{H}_{j-1}^{\perp}}\alpha_{k}+P_{\mathcal{H}_{j-1}^{\perp}}\eta_{j}=o(1).

(S.144)

By Lemma
2
, for any
n
, we can choose a linearly independent subset
\{\beta_{1}^{(n)},\ldots,\beta_{m}^{(n)}\}
from
\{P_{\mathcal{H}_{j-1}^{\perp}}\alpha_{k}:\alpha_{k}\in\mathcal{G}_{j}\setminus\mathcal{G}_{j-1}\}
such that there exists
\gamma_{1}^{(n)},\ldots,\gamma_{m}^{(n)}>0
satisfying

\displaystyle-\sum_{k:\alpha_{k}\in\mathcal{E}_{j}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}\frac{\exp(\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta_{n})}{n^{\nu_{j}}}P_{\mathcal{H}_{j-1}^{\perp}}\alpha_{k}=-\sum_{k=1}^{m}\gamma_{k}^{(n)}\beta_{k}^{(n)}.

Since the choice of
\{\beta_{1}^{(n)},\ldots,\beta_{m}^{(n)}\}
has only finite possibilities, we assume WLOG that the same set is chosen for any
n
, i.e.,
(\beta_{1}^{(n)},\ldots,\beta_{m}^{(n)})\triangleq(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{m})
for any
n
. Then we have
\sum_{k=1}^{m}\gamma_{k}^{(n)}\beta_{k}=P_{\mathcal{H}_{j-1}^{\perp}}\eta_{j}+o(1)
. Since
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{m}
are linearly independent,
(\gamma_{1}^{(n)},\ldots,\gamma_{m}^{(n)})
is bounded. We assume WLOG that
(\gamma_{1}^{(n)},\ldots,\gamma_{m}^{(n)})\rightarrow(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{m})
. This imply that
P_{\mathcal{H}_{j-1}^{\perp}}\eta_{j}=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\gamma_{k}\beta_{k}=\sum_{k=1}^{p}\gamma_{l_{k}}\beta_{l_{k}}
where
\gamma_{l_{1}},\ldots,\gamma_{l_{p}}
are strictly positive. We first choose
\beta_{l_{1}},\ldots,\beta_{l_{p}}
to enter set
\mathcal{G}
. For the rest vectors in
\mathcal{E}_{j}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{j-1}
, we rank their inner product with
\widehat{\theta}
in decreasing order and perform the following procedure: For each vector, if the vector is linearly independent with the current vectors in
\mathcal{G}
, then we let it enter set
\mathcal{G}
, otherwise we discard it. Eventually, we obtain
\mathcal{G}_{j}
and
\mathcal{H}_{j}=span\left(\mathcal{G}_{j}\right)
satisfying
\mathcal{E}_{j}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{j}
and
\eta_{j}\in\mathcal{H}_{j}
. Furthermore, we know that if vector
\alpha\in\mathcal{E}_{j}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{j-1}
is involved in the expansion of
\eta_{j}
under
\mathcal{G}_{j}
, the coefficient of
\alpha
in the expansion is strictly positive. Then by this inductive method, we obtain
\mathcal{G}_{1}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq\mathcal{G}_{J}=\mathcal{G}
. By the construction method of
\mathcal{G}_{J}
and
\mathcal{H}_{J}
, we know that
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}_{J}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{J}
and
\eta_{j}\in\mathcal{H}_{j},j=1,\ldots,J
.

Case 1:
If
card\left(\mathcal{G}\right)=d
.

For any
\alpha
in
\mathcal{G}
such that
\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}\notin\left\{\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J}\right\}
, there exists
j\in\left\{2,\ldots,J\right\}
such that
\nu_{j-1}>\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}>\nu_{j}
. (
S.144
) indicates that

\displaystyle\sum_{k:\alpha_{k}\in\mathcal{E}_{j}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}\frac{\exp(\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta_{n})}{n^{\nu_{j}}}P_{\mathcal{H}_{j-1}^{\perp}}\alpha_{k}=O(1).

(S.145)

Since
\exp(\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}\theta_{n})/n^{\nu_{j}}\gg 1
, this indicates that there exists
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{m}\in\mathcal{E}_{j}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{j-1}
such that

(i)

\beta_{1}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta},\ldots,\beta_{m}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}\geq\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}
.

(ii)

P_{\mathcal{H}_{j-1}^{\perp}}\alpha,P_{\mathcal{H}_{j-1}^{\perp}}\beta_{1},\ldots,P_{\mathcal{H}_{j-1}^{\perp}}\beta_{m}
are linearly dependent, i.e.,
\alpha\in span\left(\left\{\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{m}\right\}\cup\mathcal{G}_{j-1}\right)
.

By the construction method, before
\alpha
enter set
\mathcal{G}
, there holds
span\{\alpha_{k}:\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}>\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}\}\subseteq span\left(\mathcal{G}\right)
. This implies that for any
\beta_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}>\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta},k=1,\ldots,m
,
\beta_{k}
is already contained in
\mathcal{G}
. So there exists exactly one
\beta_{k}
among
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{m}
that did not enter set
\mathcal{G}
and satisfies
\beta^{\mathrm{T}}\theta^{(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J})}=\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}\theta^{(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J})}
.

For any
\alpha\in\mathcal{G}
such that
\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}=\nu_{j}\in\left\{\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J}\right\}
but has zero coefficient in the expansion of
\eta_{j}
under
\mathcal{G}
, we project the first order equation (
S.143
) on
span\left(\mathcal{G}\setminus\alpha\right)
and divide the equation by
n^{\nu_{j}-\delta}
, where
\delta>0
is a constant such that
\nu_{j}-\delta>\nu_{j+1}
, then we have

\displaystyle-\sum_{k:\alpha_{k}\in\mathcal{E}_{j}\setminus span\left(\mathcal{G}\setminus\alpha\right)}\frac{\exp(\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta_{n})}{n^{\nu_{j}-\delta}}P_{span^{\perp}\left(\mathcal{G}\setminus\alpha\right)}\alpha_{k}=o(1).

(S.146)

Since
\alpha\in\mathcal{E}_{j}\setminus span\left(\mathcal{G}\setminus\alpha\right)
, we have
\exp(\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}\theta_{n})/n^{\nu_{j}-\delta}\gg 1
. Similarly, we can prove that there exists
\beta\notin\mathcal{G}
such that
\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}=\beta^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}
.

Furthermore, for the above two scenarios, if we expand
\beta
in terms of basis
\mathcal{G}
, since in (
S.145
) and (
S.146
), the
\alpha
and
\beta
terms can cancel out with each other, the coefficient of
\alpha
in the expansion of
\beta
should be strictly negative.

From the construction method of
\mathcal{G}
, for any
\alpha\in\mathcal{G}
such that
\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}=\nu_{j}\in\left\{\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J}\right\}
which is involved in the expansion of
\eta_{j}
, we call
\alpha
type-1 element. Otherwise we call
\alpha
type-2 element. Then we have

(i)

For any type-1
\alpha\in\mathcal{G}
, we have
\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}=\nu_{j}\in\left\{\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J}\right\}
and the coefficient of
\alpha
in the expansion of
\eta_{j}
under
\mathcal{G}
is strictly positive. Moreover,
\eta_{j}\in span\left(\mathcal{G}\cap\mathcal{E}_{j}\right)
.

(ii)

For any type-2
\alpha\in\mathcal{G}
, there exists
\beta\notin\mathcal{G}
such that
\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}=\beta^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}
. Furthermore, the coefficient of
\alpha
in the expansion of
\beta
under
\mathcal{G}
is strictly negative.

This induces the characterization equation with solution
\widehat{\theta}
. Since the dimension of
\widehat{\theta}
matches the number of linear equations,
\widehat{\theta}
is the unique solution. Moreover, for any type-2
\alpha\in\mathcal{G}
or
\alpha\notin\mathcal{G}
, there holds
\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}\notin\left\{\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J}\right\}
. For
\beta\notin\mathcal{G}
, either
\beta^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}<\min_{\alpha\in\mathcal{G}}\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}
or there exists
\alpha\in\mathcal{G}
such that
\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}=\beta^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}\notin\left\{\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J}\right\}
. Hence all assumptions on characterization equation are verified, which proved the existence of characterization equation at
(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J})
with unique solution
\widehat{\theta}=\theta(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J})
when
k=d
.

Case 2:
If
card\left(\mathcal{G}\right)<d
.

Denote
\mathcal{E}=\{\alpha_{k}:\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}\geq 0\}
. Then we project the first order equation (
S.143
) on
\mathcal{H}_{J}
and divide by
\log n
to get:

\displaystyle-\sum_{k:\alpha_{k}\in\mathcal{E}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{J}}\frac{\exp(\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta_{n})}{\log n}P_{\mathcal{H}_{J}^{\perp}}\alpha_{k}=P_{\mathcal{H}_{J}^{\perp}}\widehat{\theta}+o(1).

Similarly, there exists
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{m}\in\mathcal{E}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{J}
and negative constants
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{m}
such that:

(i)

P_{\mathcal{H}_{J}^{\perp}}\beta_{1},\ldots,P_{\mathcal{H}_{J}^{\perp}}\beta_{m}
are linearly independent.

(ii)

P_{\mathcal{H}_{J}^{\perp}}\widehat{\theta}=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\gamma_{k}\beta_{k}
.

Similarly, we first let
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{m}
enter set
\mathcal{G}
. Then we rank the vectors in
\mathcal{E}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{J}
in decreasing order by their inner product with
\widehat{\theta}
and decide whether each vector enter set
\mathcal{G}
or not. Then we can construct
\mathcal{G}
such that
\widehat{\theta}\in span(\mathcal{G})
. Similarly we can prove that for any
\alpha\in\mathcal{G}
such that
\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}\notin\left\{\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J},0\right\}
or
\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}=0
and has zero coefficient in the expansion of
\widehat{\theta}
under basis
\mathcal{G}
, there exists
\beta\notin\mathcal{G}
such that
\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}=\beta^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}
.

If
card\left(\mathcal{G}\right)=k<d
, we have
k
equations in the characterization equation. Since we require
\widehat{\theta}\in span(\mathcal{G})
, there still exists unique solution for
(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J})
. Similar to Case 1, we can verify other conditions required for the characterization equation.

Step 3:
Now we prove the uniqueness of characterization equation at
(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J})
. We first suppose that
rank\left\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{K}\right\}=d
. Define

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}_{n}(\theta)=-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\exp(\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta)+\big(\sum_{j=1}^{J}n^{\nu_{j}}\eta_{j}\big)^{\mathrm{T}}\theta.

Step 3.1:
We first prove that for
n
large enough,
\widetilde{f}_{n}
has a unique maximizer.

For any
\epsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfying
\left\|\epsilon\right\|=1
, we discuss the two cases:

Case 1:
\max_{k=1,\ldots,K}\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon>0
. Then it is easy to show that

\displaystyle\lim_{l\rightarrow\infty}-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\exp(l\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon)+l\big(\sum_{j=1}^{J}n^{\nu_{j}}\eta_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon\big)\rightarrow-\infty.

Furthermore, we can choose
\epsilon
such that
\eta_{1}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon>0
, then for
n
large enough, it is easy to show that

\displaystyle\sup_{l\geq 0}-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\exp(l\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon)+l\big(\sum_{j=1}^{J}n^{\nu_{j}}\eta_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon\big)>0.

Case 2:
\max_{k=1,\ldots,K}\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon\leq 0
. Since
\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{J}\in X
, we have
\eta_{1}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon,\ldots,\eta_{J}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon\leq 0
. So we have

\displaystyle\sup_{l\geq 0}-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\exp(l\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon)+l\big(\sum_{j=1}^{J}n^{\nu_{j}}\eta_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon\big)\leq 0.

This implies that for
n
large enough, there exists maximizer for
\widetilde{f}_{n}
. Since
\nabla^{2}\widetilde{f}_{n}
is nonsingular, the maximizer is also unique, denoted by
\bar{\theta}_{n}
. It is easy to prove that
\bar{\theta}_{n}\neq 0
for large
n
. Then we denote
l_{n}=\left\|\bar{\theta}_{n}\right\|
and
\epsilon_{n}=\bar{\theta}_{n}/l_{n}\rightarrow\epsilon
. Note that the previous proof also implies that
\max_{k=1,\ldots,K}\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\epsilon>0
, then we can use similar method as in Step 1 to prove that
l_{n}=O(\log n)
. Assume
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\bar{\theta}_{n}/\log n\triangleq\bar{\theta}
and assume

\displaystyle\min_{k=1,\ldots,K}\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\theta}=-\widetilde{M}.

(S.147)

Step 3.2:
We expand the first order equation for
\widetilde{\theta}_{n}
in terms of the basis
\mathcal{G}
defined in characterization equation at
(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J})
.

By Step 2, there exists characterization equation at
(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J})
with unique solution
\widehat{\theta}
. We first consider the case when
k=d
. The first order equation for
\widetilde{\theta}_{n}
is

\displaystyle-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\exp(\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta_{n})\alpha_{k}+\sum_{j=1}^{J}n^{\nu_{j}}\eta_{j}=0.

(S.148)

For notation simplicity, we assume that
\{\alpha_{j_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{j_{d}}\}=\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{d}\}
in the characterization equation. Furthermore, assume that the first
l
equations are of type 1 and the other
d-l
equations are of type 2. Since by condition (i), we have
rank\left\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{d}\right\}=d
, so we expand the first order equation (
S.148
) in terms of basis
\left\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{d}\right\}
. Then we discuss the coefficient for every term
\alpha_{k},~k=1,\ldots,d
in the expansion.

For
k=1,\ldots,l
, we assume that
\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}=\nu_{j_{k}}
, where
\nu_{j_{k}}\in\left\{\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J},0\right\}
. By condition (iii), the coefficients of
\alpha_{k}
in the expansions of
\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{j_{k}-1}
are all zero. Moreover, the coefficient of
\alpha_{k}
in the expansion of
\eta_{j_{k}}
is positive. By condition (vi), for any
\alpha\in\left\{\alpha_{d+1},\ldots,\alpha_{K}\right\}
such that
\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}>\nu_{j_{k}}
, the coefficient of
\alpha_{k}
in the expansion of
\alpha
is zero. So the coefficient equation of
\alpha_{k},k=1,\ldots,l
is

\displaystyle\sum_{j=j_{k}}^{J}\gamma_{k,j}n^{\nu_{j}}-\exp(\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\theta}_{n})-\sum_{p=d+1,\ldots,K:\alpha_{p}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}<\nu_{j_{k}}}\xi_{k,p}\exp(\alpha_{p}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\theta}_{n})=0,

(S.149)

where
\gamma_{k,j_{k}}>0
and
\xi_{k,p}
is the coefficient of
\alpha_{k}
in the expansion of
\alpha_{p}
for
p=d+1,\ldots,K
.

For
k=l+1,\ldots,J
, we assume that
\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}\in(\nu_{j_{k}},\nu_{j_{k}-1})
(define
\nu_{J+1}=0
). By condition (iii), the coefficient of
\alpha_{k}
in the expansion of
\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{j_{k}-1}
is zero. By conditions (v) and (vi), there exists unique
\alpha_{p_{k}}\in\left\{\alpha_{d+1},\ldots,\alpha_{K}\right\}
such that
\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}=\alpha_{p_{k}}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}
. Moreover, the coefficient of
\alpha_{k}
in the expansion of
\alpha_{p_{k}}
is negative. For any
\beta\in\left\{\alpha_{d+1},\ldots,\alpha_{K}\right\}
such that
\beta^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}>\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}
, the coefficient of
\alpha_{k}
in the expansion of
\beta
is zero. So the coefficient equation of
\alpha_{k},k=l+1,\ldots,J
is

\displaystyle\sum_{j=j_{k}}^{J}\gamma_{k,j}n^{\nu_{j}}-\exp(\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\theta}_{n})-\xi_{k}\exp(\alpha_{p_{k}}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\theta}_{n})-\sum_{p=d+1,\ldots,K:\alpha_{p}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}<\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}}\xi_{k,p}\exp(\alpha_{p}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\theta}_{n})=0,

(S.150)

where
\xi_{k}<0
is the coefficient of
\alpha_{k}
in the expansion of
\alpha_{p_{k}}
and
\xi_{k,p}
is the coefficient of
\alpha_{k}
in the expansion of
\alpha_{p}
for
p=d+1,\ldots,K
.

Step 3.3:
We expand
\widetilde{f}_{n}(\widetilde{\theta}_{n})
into infinite series.

We first consider the case when
k=d
. We match the term of highest order each time. We first find the solution
\theta_{n}^{(1)}
to the equations matching the terms with highest order in (
S.149
) and (
S.150
), which are

(i)

For
k=1,\ldots,l
, there holds
\gamma_{k,j_{k}}n^{\nu_{j_{k}}}-\exp(\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta_{n}^{(1)})=0
.

(ii)

For
k=l+1,\ldots,d
, there holds
-\exp(\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta_{n}^{(1)})-\xi_{k}\exp(\alpha_{p_{k}}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta_{n}^{(1)})=0
.

Since
\gamma_{k,j_{k}}>0
for
k=1,\ldots,l
and
\xi_{k}<0
for
k=l+1,\ldots,J
, there exists unique solution and it is easy to prove that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\theta_{n}^{(1)}}{\log n}=\widehat{\theta}.

So for every
k=1,\ldots,K
, there holds
\exp(\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta_{n}^{(1)})=c_{k}n^{\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}}
where
c_{1},\ldots,c_{K}
are positive constants.

Now we calculate
\nabla\widetilde{f}_{n}(\theta_{n}^{(1)})
