to get the residual terms:

(i)

For
k=1,\ldots,l
, the residual terms are:

\displaystyle\nabla\widetilde{f}_{n}(\theta_{n}^{(1)})=\sum_{j=j_{k}+1}^{J}\gamma_{k,j}n^{\nu_{j}}-\sum_{p=d+1,\ldots,K:\alpha_{p}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}<\nu_{j_{k}}}\xi_{k,p}\exp(\alpha_{p}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta_{n}^{(1)}).

(ii)

For
k=l+1,\ldots,d
, the residual terms are:

\displaystyle\nabla\widetilde{f}_{n}(\theta_{n}^{(1)})=\sum_{j=j_{k}}^{J}\gamma_{k,j}n^{\nu_{j}}-\sum_{p=d+1,\ldots,K:\alpha_{p}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}<\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}}\xi_{k,p}\exp(\alpha_{p}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta_{n}^{(1)}).

Noticing that all terms in the residual are of the form
cn^{\xi}
. In the following proof, we define the order of terms with form
cn^{\xi}
or
cn^{\xi}\log n
as
\xi
. Then we can define the order gap for each residual:

(i)

For
k=1,\ldots,l
, the order gap
\delta_{k}^{(1)}
is defined as the difference between the highest order in the residual with
\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\delta_{k}^{(1)}=\nu_{j_{k}}-\big(\max_{j=j_{k}+1,\ldots,J}\nu_{j}\big)\vee\big(\max_{p=d+1,\ldots,K:\alpha_{p}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}<\nu_{j_{k}}}\alpha_{p}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}\big).

(ii)

For
k=l+1,\ldots,d
, the order gap
\delta_{k}^{(1)}
is defined as the difference between the highest order in the residual with
\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\delta_{k}^{(1)}=\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}-\big(\max_{j=j_{k},\ldots,J}\nu_{j}\big)\vee\big(\max_{p=d+1,\ldots,K:\alpha_{p}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}<\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}}\alpha_{p}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}\big).

We assume WLOG that
\delta_{1}^{(1)},\ldots,\delta_{d}^{(1)}
all exists and are finite. Suppose
\delta_{m}^{(1)}=\min_{l=1,\ldots,d}\delta_{l}^{(1)}
and suppose
q
is the smallest integer such that
q\delta_{m}^{(1)}>\max_{l=1,\ldots,d}\delta_{l}^{(1)}
. For
k=1,\ldots,d
, let

\displaystyle\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta_{n}^{(2)}=\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta_{n}^{(1)}+\sum_{p=1}^{q}c_{k,p}n^{-p\delta_{m}^{(1)}}.

(S.151)

Then we determine
(c_{1,1},\ldots,c_{d,1}),\ldots,(c_{1,q},\ldots,c_{d,q})
in (
S.151
) by the following inductive method:

For
p=1
, we expand
\exp(\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta_{n}^{(1)}+c_{k,1}n^{-\delta_{m}^{(1)}})
to the first order and find
(c_{1,1},\ldots,c_{d,1})
to cancel all terms in every residual with order gap
\delta_{m}^{(1)}
. So
(c_{1,1},\ldots,c_{d,1})
can be obtained by solving the following equation:

(i)

For
k=1,\ldots,l
,
c_{k,1}=\beta_{k}
, where
\beta_{k}
is a constant depending on the constants in (
S.149
) and (
S.150
).

(ii)

For
k=l+1,\ldots,d
,
c_{k,1}-(\sum_{l=1}^{d}\xi_{l,k}c_{l,1})=\beta_{k}
, where
\beta_{k}
is a constant depending on the constants in (
S.149
) and (
S.150
) and
\alpha_{p_{k}}=\sum_{l=1}^{d}\xi_{l,k}\alpha_{l}
.

We can write the above linear equations in matrix form:
\Xi c=\beta
, where
\Xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and
c,\beta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Noticing that the characterization equation can be written as:

\displaystyle 0=\left(\alpha_{1}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta},\ldots,\alpha_{l}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta},\alpha_{l+1}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}-\alpha_{p_{l+1}}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta},\ldots,\alpha_{d}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}-\alpha_{p_{d}}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}\right)=\Xi\left(\alpha_{1}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta},\ldots,\alpha_{d}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}\right)=\Xi\left(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{d}\right)^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}.

Since
\left(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{d}\right)
is invertible and the characterization equation has unique solution by condition (ii),
\Xi
is invertible. So equation
\Xi c=\beta
has unique solution. So we match the terms in each residual with order gap
\delta_{m}^{(1)}
.

If we have matched the terms in each residual with order gap
\delta_{m}^{(1)},\ldots,(p-1)\delta_{m}^{(1)}
, then we expand
\exp(\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta_{n}^{(1)}+\sum_{l=1}^{p}c_{k,l}n^{-l\delta_{m}^{(1)}})
to cancel out the terms with order gap
p\delta_{m}^{(1)}
. Similarly, we can prove that there exists unique solution for
(c_{1,p},\ldots,c_{d,p})
. So by this inductive method, we obtain solution
\theta_{n}^{(2)}
such that all terms with order gap
\delta_{m}^{(1)},\ldots,q\delta_{m}^{(1)}
are canceled out in each residual. By the construction method of
\theta_{n}^{(2)}
, we have
\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta_{n}^{(1)}-\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta_{n}^{(2)}=o(1)
for any
k=1,\ldots,K
, so we have
\theta_{n}^{(1)}-\theta_{n}^{(2)}=o(1)
, which indicates that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\theta_{n}^{(2)}}{\log n}=\widehat{\theta}.

This implies that the order of each term in the residual will not change within finite procedures. Then by the assumption on integer
q
, the order gap in the
m
-th residual has decreased strictly, while the order gaps in other residuals have remained the same. Since the order gap of all terms in residuals can only be the linear combination of the order gaps in the residuals obtained in the first approximation with nonnegative integer coefficients, we can reduce the highest order in all residuals to any given level in finite steps.

Now assume that

\displaystyle\min_{k=1,\ldots,K}\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}=-M,

(S.152)

then for any given constant
C>0
, suppose that we obtain
\theta_{n}^{(L)}
over
L
procedures satisfying

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla\widetilde{f}_{n}(\theta_{n}^{(L)})\right\|\lesssim n^{-2d-d\left(M\vee\widetilde{M}\right)-C/2}.

(S.153)

By similar proof in Proposition
4
, we have

\displaystyle\max_{k=1,\ldots,K}\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}=\max_{k=1,\ldots,K}\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\theta}=1.

(S.154)

Then by (
S.147
), (
S.152
) and (
S.154
), for
n
large enough we have

\displaystyle n^{-\widetilde{M}-1}\lesssim\min_{k=1,\ldots,K}\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\theta}_{n}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{k=1,\ldots,K}\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\theta}_{n}\lesssim n^{2},

\displaystyle n^{-M-1}\lesssim\min_{k=1,\ldots,K}\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta_{n}^{(L)}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{k=1,\ldots,K}\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta_{n}^{(L)}\lesssim n^{2}.

Then for
n
large enough we have

\displaystyle n^{-d\left(\widetilde{M}+1\right)}\lesssim

\displaystyle\big\|-\nabla^{2}\widetilde{f}_{n}(\theta_{n})\big\|_{2}\lesssim n^{2d},

\displaystyle n^{-d\left({M}+1\right)}\lesssim

\displaystyle\big\|-\nabla^{2}\widetilde{f}_{n}(\theta_{n}^{(L)})\big\|_{2}\lesssim n^{2d}.

(S.155)

By Taylor expansion, we have

\displaystyle 0=\nabla\widetilde{f}_{n}(\widetilde{\theta}_{n})=\nabla\widetilde{f}_{n}(\theta_{n}^{(L)})+\nabla^{2}\widetilde{f}_{n}\left(\theta_{n}^{\ast}\right)(\widetilde{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{n}^{(L)}),

(S.156)

where
\theta^{\ast}_{n}
is a point between
\widetilde{\theta}_{n}
and
\theta_{n}^{(L)}
. By (
S.153
), (
S.2.10
) and (
S.156
), we have

\displaystyle n^{-d-d(M\vee\widetilde{M})}\big\|\theta_{n}-\theta_{n}^{(L)}\big\|\lesssim\big\|-\nabla^{2}\widetilde{f}_{n}(\theta_{n}^{\ast})(\widetilde{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{n}^{(L)})\big\|=\big\|\nabla\widetilde{f}_{n}(\theta_{n}^{(L)})\big\|\lesssim n^{-2d-d(M\vee\widetilde{M})-C/2}.

This implies that
\|\widetilde{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{n}^{(L)}\|\lesssim n^{-d-C/2}
. Then by (
S.2.10
),

\displaystyle\big|\widetilde{f}_{n}(\theta_{n}^{(L)})-\widetilde{f}_{n}(\theta_{n})\big|=\left|\frac{1}{2}(\theta_{n}^{(L)}-\theta_{n})^{\mathrm{T}}\left(-\nabla^{2}\widetilde{f}_{n}(\theta_{n}^{\ast})\right)(\theta_{n}^{(L)}-\theta_{n})\right|\lesssim n^{2d-2d-C}=n^{-C}.

Furthermore, by the construction method of
\theta_{n}^{(L)}
, all terms in the taylor series of
\widetilde{f}_{n}(\theta_{n}^{(L)})
are of the form
cn^{\xi}
or
cn^{\xi}\log n
, where the coefficients are functions depending on
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{K}
and
\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J}
and the power is the linear combination of
\alpha_{1}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta},\ldots,\alpha_{K}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}
with integer coefficients.

If
k<d
. Define

\displaystyle\bar{f}_{n}(\theta)=-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\exp(\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta)+\sum_{j=1}^{J}n^{\nu_{j}}\eta_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta+\sum_{p=1,\ldots,k:\alpha_{p}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}=0}\alpha_{p}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta

and denote its maximizer by
\bar{\theta}_{n}
. The additional term
\sum_{p=1,\ldots,k:\alpha_{p}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}=0}\alpha_{p}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta
ensures that the equation matching the terms with highest order has a solution. Similarly we can prove that
\bar{\theta}_{n}=O(\log n)
, then we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}_{n}(\widetilde{\theta}_{n})-\bar{f}_{n}(\bar{\theta}_{n})\leq

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}_{n}(\widetilde{\theta}_{n})-\bar{f}_{n}(\widetilde{\theta}_{n})=-\sum_{p=1,\ldots,k:\alpha_{p}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}=0}\alpha_{p}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\theta}_{n}\lesssim\log n,

\displaystyle\bar{f}_{n}(\bar{\theta}_{n})-\widetilde{f}_{n}(\widetilde{\theta}_{n})\leq

\displaystyle\bar{f}_{n}(\bar{\theta}_{n})-\widetilde{f}_{n}(\bar{\theta}_{n})=\sum_{p=1,\ldots,k:\alpha_{p}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}=0}\alpha_{p}^{\mathrm{T}}\bar{\theta}_{n}\lesssim\log n.

This indicates that
|\widetilde{f}_{n}(\widetilde{\theta}_{n})-\bar{f}_{n}(\bar{\theta}_{n})|\lesssim\log n
. Hence substituting
\widetilde{f}_{n}
by
\bar{f}_{n}
will not lead to error of positive order for the maximum value. We then use similar method to approximate
\widetilde{f}_{n}(\widetilde{\theta}_{n})
by the solution of characterization equation. The only difference is that we requires solution
\theta_{n}^{(L)}\in span\left\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}\right\}
for any
L\in\mathbb{N}
. Similarly, we assume that after
\widetilde{L}
procedures, we have
|\bar{f}_{n}(\bar{\theta}_{n}^{(\widetilde{L})})-\bar{f}_{n}(\bar{\theta}_{n})|\lesssim n^{-C}
.

Step 3.4:
We prove the uniqueness of characterization equation at
(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J})
.

If there exists two characterization equations at
(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J})
with solutions
\theta
and
\widetilde{\theta}
respectively, we assume WLOG that
k=d
in both cases for simplicity since we only need to match the terms with positive order. Then the same function
\widetilde{f}_{n}
is denoted in both cases. By procedure in Step 3.3, there exists finite
L
such that

\displaystyle\big|\widetilde{f}_{n}(\theta_{n}^{(L)})-\widetilde{f}_{n}(\widetilde{\theta}_{n}^{(L)})\big|\leq\big|\widetilde{f}_{n}(\theta_{n}^{(L)})-\widetilde{f}_{n}(\widetilde{\theta}_{n})\big|+\big|\widetilde{f}_{n}(\widetilde{\theta}_{n})-\widetilde{f}_{n}(\widetilde{\theta}_{n}^{(L)})\big|\lesssim\log n.

Since the taylor series of
\widetilde{f}_{n}(\theta_{n}^{(L)})
and
\widetilde{f}_{n}(\widetilde{\theta}_{n}^{(L)})
consist of terms of the form
cn^{\xi}
or
cn^{\xi}\log n
, where the coefficients are functions depending on
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{K}
and
\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J}
and the power is the linear combination of
\alpha_{1}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta},\ldots,\alpha_{K}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}
with integer coefficients, for both coefficient and power should match exactly for terms with order greater than 0, which indicates that all positive terms among
\alpha_{1}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta,\ldots,\alpha_{K}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta
should match with all positive terms among
\alpha_{1}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\theta},\ldots,\alpha_{K}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\theta}
exactly. Then the problem falls into two cases:

Case 1:
If at least one of the two characterization equations contains type-2 equations, we assume WLOG that the characterization equation for
\theta
contains type-2 equation:
\alpha_{j_{l}}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta=\zeta_{l}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta
. By assumption (v),
\alpha_{j_{l}}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta>0
. Then the two characterization equations should match exactly, or the term with order
\alpha_{j_{l}}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta
can not be matched. So
\theta=\widetilde{\theta}
.

Case 2:
If both characterization equations contains only type-1 equations, then by Lemma
3
, the two characterization equations should match exactly and
\theta=\widetilde{\theta}
.

If
rank\left\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{K}\right\}<d
, we change variables to reduce dimension to
rank\left\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{K}\right\}
. Then the same proof is performed. Hence the uniqueness of characterization equation is proved.

Step 4:
Finally, we prove Lemma
4
by the uniqueness of characterization equation. Since for any
\{(\xi_{1}^{(n)},\ldots,\xi_{J}^{(n)})\}
such that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{(\log\xi_{1}^{(n)},\ldots,\log\xi_{J}^{(n)})}{\log n}=(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J}),

we have
\xi_{1}^{(n)}\gg\ldots\gg\xi_{J}^{(n)}\gg 1
. Then we can use the same method as in part (2) to construct characterization equation at
(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J})
. So by uniqueness of characterization equation at
(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J})
,
\theta_{n}/\log n
should converge to the same limit
\theta(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J})
. Since
\nu_{1}>\ldots>\nu_{J}>0
, by changing
(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J})
in a small neighborhood, the equation still satisfy all the conditions for the characterization equation. By the continuity of linear equation, the solution should also be continuous when changing
(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J})
in a small neighborhood. This implies that
\theta(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J})
is continuous at
(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J})
. Hence the lemma is proved.
∎

Proof of Proposition
8
.

Similar to Lemma
4
, we can prove that
\theta_{n}=O(\log n)
and denote
\theta_{n}/\log n\rightarrow\theta(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J})
. It is easy to prove that the existence of
\varphi^{(n)}
will lead to error of order
o(1)
in the maximum point, which will not affect the limit of
\theta_{n}/\log n
. Since the number of characterization equation is finite if we omit the particular value of
\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J}
, we can assume WLOG that the characterization equation have the same structure at
(\nu_{1}^{(n)},\ldots,\nu_{J}^{(n)})
except for the values of
(\nu_{1}^{(n)},\ldots,\nu_{J}^{(n)})
are different. Since
(\nu_{1}^{(n)},\ldots,\nu_{J}^{(n)})\rightarrow(\widetilde{\nu}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{\nu}_{J})
, we can derive a limit linear equation by letting
n
goes to infinity. Since by assumption, the expansions of
\eta_{i}
and
\eta_{j}
under the basis of characterization contain disjoint terms, this implies that the limit linear equation is a valid characterization equation in a small neighborhood of
(\widetilde{\nu}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{\nu}_{J})
if we allow ties among
(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J})
. By the same uniqueness argument as in the proof of Lemma
4
, we can show that the equation is the unique characterization equation in the neighborhood
\mathcal{O}
which correspond to the maximum point. Then by same argument as in Lemma
4
, the result is proved.
∎

S.3
Proof of Theorem
2

For notation simplicity, we assume WLOG that
\Sigma_{0}=I_{K}
and let
A
absorb the transformation on
\Sigma_{0}
.

S.3.1
Preliminary Results

We first state some preliminary results to be used in the proof of Theorem
2
. The proof of
these results are given in subsequent sections.

Proposition 9
.

If the Fisher information matrix is singular at
\delta_{0}=(\beta_{0},A_{0},\Sigma_{0})
, then there exists nonzero
w=\{u_{j0}\in\mathbb{R},u_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{L_{1}},V_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{L_{2}\times K}:j=1,\ldots,J\}
such that for any
t\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\int\left[\sum_{j=1}^{J}\int_{0}^{t}(u_{j0}+u_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}X_{j}(s)+\theta^{\mathrm{T}}V_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}Z_{j}(s))(dN_{j}(s)-\lambda_{j}(s)ds)\right]

\displaystyle\times\prod_{j=1}^{J}\Big[\prod_{s\leq t}(\lambda_{j}(s)^{\Delta N_{j}(s)})e^{-\int_{0}^{\mathrm{t}}\lambda_{j}(s)ds}\Big]\left(\sum_{j=1}^{J}\lambda_{j}(t+0)\right)^{n}\phi_{K}(\theta;0,I_{K})d\theta

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\int\left[\sum_{j=1}^{J}(u_{j0}+u_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}X_{j}(t+0)+\theta^{\mathrm{T}}V_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}Z_{j}(t))\lambda_{j}(t)\right]

\displaystyle\times\prod_{j=1}^{J}\Big[\prod_{s\leq t}(\lambda_{j}(s)^{\Delta N_{j}(s)})e^{-\int_{0}^{\mathrm{t}}\lambda_{j}(s)ds}\Big]\left(\sum_{j=1}^{J}\lambda_{j}(t+0)\right)^{n-1}\phi_{K}(\theta;0,I_{K})d\theta

(S.157)

and for each
m\in\{1,\ldots,J\}
,

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\int\left[\sum_{j=1}^{J}\int_{0}^{t}(u_{j0}+u_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}X_{j}(s)+\theta^{\mathrm{T}}V_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}Z_{j}(s))(dN_{j}(s)-\lambda_{j}(s)ds)+u_{m0}+u_{m}^{\mathrm{T}}X_{m}(t+0)+\theta^{\mathrm{T}}V_{m}^{\mathrm{T}}Z_{m}(t+0)\right]

\displaystyle\times\lambda_{m}(t+0)\prod_{j=1}^{J}\Big[\prod_{s\leq t}(\lambda_{j}(s)^{\Delta N_{j}(s)})e^{-\int_{0}^{\mathrm{t}}\lambda_{j}(s)ds}\Big]\left(\sum_{j=1}^{J}\lambda_{j}(t+0)\right)^{n}\phi_{K}(\theta;0,I_{K})d\theta

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\int\left[\sum_{j=1}^{J}(u_{j0}+u_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}X_{j}(t+0)+\theta^{\mathrm{T}}V_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}Z_{j}(t))\lambda_{j}(t)\right]

\displaystyle\times\lambda_{m}(t+0)\prod_{j=1}^{J}\Big[\prod_{s\leq t}(\lambda_{j}(s)^{\Delta N_{j}(s)})e^{-\int_{0}^{\mathrm{t}}\lambda_{j}(s)ds}\Big]\left(\sum_{j=1}^{J}\lambda_{j}(t+0)\right)^{n-1}\phi_{K}(\theta;0,I_{K})d\theta~~\text{a.s.}

(S.158)

Proposition 10
.

Let
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{K},\{\xi_{n}\},\gamma\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be
d
-vectors and
\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{K}
be positive constants. Define
f_{n}(\theta)=-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\exp(\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta)+\xi_{n}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta-\frac{1}{2}\theta^{\mathrm{T}}\theta
and denote its unique maximum point by
\widehat{\theta}_{n}
. Suppose
\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}_{n}\rightarrow\infty
(or
\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}_{n}\rightarrow-\infty
). Denote the negative Hessian matrix of function
f_{n}
at
\theta
by
I(\theta)=I_{d}+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\exp(\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta)\alpha_{k}\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}
. Then there holds

\displaystyle M^{-1}\frac{(\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}_{n})\exp(f_{n}(\widehat{\theta}_{n}))}{\sqrt{\operatorname{det}(I(\widehat{\theta}_{n}))}}\leq\int(2\pi)^{d/2}(\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}\theta)\exp(f_{n}(\theta))d\theta\leq M\frac{(\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}_{n})\exp(f_{n}(\widehat{\theta}_{n}))}{\sqrt{\operatorname{det}(I(\widehat{\theta}_{n}))}},

where
M>0
is a constant that does not depend on
n
.

S.3.2
Main Proof of Theorem
2

Proof of Theorem
2
.

We first show that
I(\alpha_{0})
is finite. The complete log-likelihood is

\displaystyle\log L(\alpha_{0}|\textbf{N},\textbf{X},\textbf{Z},\theta)=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{J}\int^{T}_{0}\big(\beta_{j0}+\beta^{\mathrm{T}}_{j}X_{j}(t)+\theta^{\mathrm{T}}A_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}Z_{j}(t)\big)dN_{j}(t)

\displaystyle-\sum_{j=1}^{J}\int^{T}_{0}\exp\left(\beta_{j0}+\beta^{\mathrm{T}}_{j}X_{j}(t)+\theta^{\mathrm{T}}A_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}Z_{j}(t)\right)dt.

For any nonzero
w=\{u_{j0}\in\mathbb{R},u_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{L_{1}},V_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{L_{2}\times D}:j=1,\ldots,J\}
, the score function in direction
w
is as

\displaystyle l_{w}=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\int_{0}^{T}(u_{j0}+u_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}X_{j}(t)+\theta^{\mathrm{T}}V_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}Z_{j}(t))(dN_{j}(t)-\lambda_{j}(t)dt).

By the law of total variance, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{var}\left(\left\{\frac{\partial}{\partial\alpha}\log L(\alpha|\textbf{N},\textbf{X},\textbf{Z})\right\}^{\mathrm{T}}w\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{var}_{\textbf{N},\textbf{X},\textbf{Z}}\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\left(l_{w}|\textbf{N},\textbf{X},\textbf{Z}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\operatorname{var}(l_{w})

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathbb{E}\int\int_{0}^{\tau}(u_{j0}+u_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}X_{j}(t)+\theta^{\mathrm{T}}V_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}Z_{j}(t))^{2}\exp\left(\beta_{j0}+\beta^{\mathrm{T}}_{j}X_{j}(t)+\theta^{\mathrm{T}}A_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}Z_{j}(t)\right)dt

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C,

where
\tau>0
is the duration of the study. Here
C>0
is a constant since
X,Z
are bounded by
M>0
due to Condition (b). Since the choice of
w
is arbitrary,
I(\alpha_{0})
is finite.

Now we use method of contradiction to prove Theorem
2
. For notation simplicity, we denote
\mu_{j}(t)=\beta_{j0}+\beta_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}X_{j}(t)
. Now we fix an arbitrary trajectory with positive density. Then by Condition (e),
[0,T]
can be divided into
v
finite intervals:
[0,t_{1}],(t_{1},t_{2}],\ldots,(t_{v-1},t_{v}]
such that the values of
X
and
Z
are constant on each interval. We then use induction method to prove that for any
j,j_{1},j_{2}
and
0\leq t,s\leq T
, there holds

\displaystyle u_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}X_{j}(t)

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle(V_{j_{1}}^{\mathrm{T}}Z_{j_{1}}(t))^{\mathrm{T}}(A_{j_{2}}^{\mathrm{T}}Z_{j_{2}}(s))

\displaystyle=0.

(S.159)

We first prove that (
S.3.2
) holds on interval
[0,t_{1}]
. We choose
t=0
in Proposition
9
to get

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\int\left[\sum_{j=1}^{J}(u_{j0}+u_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}X_{j}(0)+\theta^{\mathrm{T}}V_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}Z_{j}(0))\lambda_{j}(0)\right]\left(\sum_{j=1}^{J}\lambda_{j}(0)\right)^{n}\phi_{K}(\theta;0,I)d\theta

(S.160)

By explicit integration of (
S.160
) we have

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{1\leq j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}\leq J}\exp\Bigg(\mu_{j}(0)+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\mu_{j_{k}}(0)+\frac{1}{2}\Big[\sum_{k=1}^{n}A^{\mathrm{T}}_{j_{k}}Z_{j_{k}}(0)+A^{\mathrm{T}}_{j}Z_{j}(0)\Big]^{\mathrm{T}}

\displaystyle\Big[\sum_{k=1}^{n}A^{\mathrm{T}}_{j_{k}}Z_{j_{k}}(0)+A^{\mathrm{T}}_{j}Z_{j}(0)\Big]\Bigg)\left(u_{j0}+u_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}X_{j}(0)+\Big[\sum_{k=1}^{n}A^{\mathrm{T}}_{j_{k}}Z_{j_{k}}(0)+A^{\mathrm{T}}_{j}Z_{j}(0)\Big]^{\mathrm{T}}V_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}Z_{j}(0)\right).

(S.161)

We assume WLOG that
Z_{1}(0),\ldots,Z_{J}(0)
are all nonzero. By excluding a zero measure set in the parameter space, we assume WLOG that
\{(A^{\mathrm{T}}_{j_{1}}Z_{j_{1}}(0))^{\mathrm{T}}A^{\mathrm{T}}_{j_{2}}Z_{j_{2}}(0):1\leq j_{1}\leq j_{2}\leq J\}
are distinct and assume that
(A^{\mathrm{T}}_{1}Z_{1}(0))^{\mathrm{T}}A^{\mathrm{T}}_{1}Z_{1}(0)>\max_{j=2,\ldots,J}(A^{\mathrm{T}}_{j}Z_{j}(0))^{\mathrm{T}}A^{\mathrm{T}}_{j}Z_{j}(0)
. Furthermore, we assume WLOG that
(A^{\mathrm{T}}_{1}Z_{1}(0))^{\mathrm{T}}A^{\mathrm{T}}_{1}Z_{1}(0)>\ldots>(A^{\mathrm{T}}_{1}Z_{1}(0))^{\mathrm{T}}A^{\mathrm{T}}_{J}Z_{J}(0)
.

Similar to the proof in Lemma
2
, we can rank all terms in the right hand side of (
S.3.2
) and prove that each term dominates the summation of all terms with lower order. For example, if
(A^{\mathrm{T}}_{1}Z_{1}(0))^{\mathrm{T}}V_{1}^{\mathrm{T}}Z_{1}(0)\neq 0
, we can show that

\displaystyle(n+1)\exp\left((n+1)\mu_{1}(0)+\frac{(n+1)^{2}}{2}(A^{\mathrm{T}}_{1}Z_{1}(0))^{\mathrm{T}}A^{\mathrm{T}}_{1}Z_{1}(0)\right)(A^{\mathrm{T}}_{1}Z_{1}(0))^{\mathrm{T}}V_{1}^{\mathrm{T}}Z_{1}(0)

dominates the right hand side of (
S.3.2
), which leads to contradiction. Hence
(A^{\mathrm{T}}_{1}Z_{1}(0))^{\mathrm{T}}V_{1}^{\mathrm{T}}Z_{1}(0)=0
. Then we can prove that

\displaystyle\exp\left((n+1)\mu_{1}(0)+\frac{(n+1)^{2}}{2}(A^{\mathrm{T}}_{1}Z_{1}(0))^{\mathrm{T}}A^{\mathrm{T}}_{1}Z_{1}(0)\right)(u_{10}+u_{1}^{\mathrm{T}}X_{1}(0))

dominates the right hand side of (
S.3.2
) if
u_{10}+u_{1}^{\mathrm{T}}X_{1}(0)\neq 0
. Hence
u_{10}+u_{1}^{\mathrm{T}}X_{1}(0)=0
. By this inductive method, we can show that for any
j,j_{1},j_{2}=1,\ldots,J
, we have

u_{j0}+u_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}X_{j}(0)=0
and
(A^{\mathrm{T}}_{j_{1}}Z_{j_{1}}(0))^{\mathrm{T}}V_{j_{2}}^{\mathrm{T}}Z_{j_{2}}(0)=0
, which finishes the proof on
[0,t_{1}]
.

Now suppose that (
S.3.2
) is proved on interval
[0,t_{q}]
, we then prove that (
S.3.2
) also holds on interval
[0,t_{q+1}]
. By applying
t=t_{q}
in Proposition
9
, we have

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\int\left[\sum_{j=1}^{J}\int_{0}^{t_{q}}(u_{j0}+u_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}X_{j}(s)+\theta^{\mathrm{T}}V_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}Z_{j}(s))(dN_{j}(s)-\lambda_{j}(s)ds)\right]

\displaystyle\times\prod_{j=1}^{J}\Big[\prod_{s\leq t_{q}}(\lambda_{j}(s)^{\Delta N_{j}(s)})e^{-\int_{0}^{t_{q}}\lambda_{j}(s)ds}\Big]\left(\sum_{j=1}^{J}\lambda_{j}(t_{q+1})\right)^{n}\phi_{K}(\theta;0,I)d\theta

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\int\left[\sum_{j=1}^{J}(u_{j0}+u_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}X_{j}(t_{q+1})+\theta^{\mathrm{T}}V_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}Z_{j}(t_{q+1}))\lambda_{j}(t_{q+1})\right]

\displaystyle\times\prod_{j=1}^{J}\Big[\prod_{s\leq t_{q}}(\lambda_{j}(s)^{\Delta N_{j}(s)})e^{-\int_{0}^{t_{q}}\lambda_{j}(s)ds}\Big]\left(\sum_{j=1}^{J}\lambda_{j}(t_{q+1})\right)^{n-1}\phi_{K}(\theta;0,I)d\theta.

(S.162)

Denote
t_{0}=0
. To simplify the notation, for any
k=0,\ldots,q-1,~j=1\ldots,J
, we introduce the following notations:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\varphi=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\int_{0}^{t_{q}}A^{\mathrm{T}}_{j}Z_{j}(t)dN_{j}(t),&\widetilde{\varphi}=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\int_{0}^{t_{q}}V^{\mathrm{T}}_{j}Z_{j}(t)dN_{j}(t)\\
\alpha_{kJ+j}=A^{\mathrm{T}}_{j}Z_{j}(t_{k+1}),&\widetilde{\alpha}_{kJ+j}=V^{\mathrm{T}}_{j}Z_{j}(t_{k+1})\\
\omega_{kJ+j}=\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}\exp(\mu_{j}(s)ds),&\widetilde{\mu}_{j}=u_{j0}+u_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}X_{j}(t_{q+1})\\
\eta_{j}=A^{\mathrm{T}}_{j}Z_{j}(t_{q+1}),&\widetilde{\eta}_{j}=V^{\mathrm{T}}_{j}Z_{j}(t_{q+1}).\end{array}

Denote
W=qJ
. For any
n
and
\bm{\xi}^{(n)}=(\xi_{2}^{(n)},\ldots,\xi_{J}^{(n)})
we introduce the following notations:

\displaystyle f_{n}(\theta\big|\bm{\xi}^{(n)})

\displaystyle=n\mu_{1}-\sum_{k=1}^{W}\omega_{k}\exp(\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta)+(\varphi+n\eta_{1})^{\mathrm{T}}\theta-\frac{1}{2}\theta^{\mathrm{T}}\theta-\sum_{j=2}^{J}\xi_{j}^{(n)}\left[(\eta_{1}-\eta_{j})^{\mathrm{T}}\theta+(\mu_{1}-\mu_{j})\right],

\displaystyle\phi_{n}(\bm{\xi}^{(n)})

\displaystyle=\int(2\pi)^{-\frac{K}{2}}\exp\left(f_{n}(\theta\big|\bm{\xi}^{(n)})\right)d\theta,

\displaystyle\Delta_{n}(\bm{\xi}^{(n)})

\displaystyle=\binom{n}{n-\sum_{j=2}^{J}\xi_{j}^{(n)},\xi_{2}^{(n)},\ldots,\xi_{J}^{(n)}}=\frac{n!}{\left(n-\sum_{j=2}^{J}\xi_{j}^{(n)}\right)!\prod_{j=2}^{J}\xi_{j}^{(n)}!}.

Furthermore, denote the unique maximizer of
{f}_{n}(\theta\big|\bm{\xi}^{(n)})
by
\theta_{n}(\bm{\xi}^{(n)})
. For any
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, define
\mathcal{O}_{n}=\{(\xi_{2},\ldots,\xi_{J})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{J-1}:\sum_{j=2}^{J}\xi_{j}\leq n\}
. By induction assumption, we have
\alpha_{k_{1}}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\alpha}_{k_{2}}=\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\varphi}=\widetilde{\alpha}_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}{\varphi}=\varphi^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\varphi}=u_{j0}+u_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}X_{j}(t)=0
for any
k,k_{1},k_{2}=1,\ldots,W
,
j=1,\ldots,J
and
0\leq t\leq t_{q}
. Then equation (
S.3.2
) can be explicitly characterized as

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\sum_{\bm{\xi}^{(n)}\in\mathcal{O}_{n}}\widetilde{\varphi}^{\mathrm{T}}\left(n\eta_{1}-\sum_{j=2}^{J}\xi_{j}^{(n)}(\eta_{1}-\eta_{j})\right)\Delta_{n}(\bm{\xi}^{(n)})\phi_{n}(\bm{\xi}^{(n)})

\displaystyle-\sum_{\bm{\xi}^{(n)}\in\mathcal{O}_{n}}\sum_{k=1}^{W}\omega_{k}\widetilde{\alpha}_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\left(n\eta_{1}-\sum_{j=2}^{J}\xi_{j}^{(n)}(\eta_{1}-\eta_{j})\right)\Delta_{n}(\bm{\xi}^{(n)})\int(2\pi)^{-\frac{K}{2}}\exp\left(f_{n}(\theta\big|\bm{\xi}^{(n)})+\alpha_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta\right)d\theta

\displaystyle-\sum_{j=1}^{J}\widetilde{\mu}_{j}\exp(\mu_{j})\sum_{\bm{\xi}^{(n-1)}\in\mathcal{O}_{n-1}}\Delta_{n-1}(\bm{\xi}^{(n-1)})\int(2\pi)^{-\frac{K}{2}}\exp\left(f_{n-1}(\theta\big|\bm{\xi}^{(n-1)})+\eta_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta\right)d\theta

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{k=1}^{W}\omega_{k}\exp(\mu_{j})\widetilde{\eta}_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}\alpha_{k}\sum_{\bm{\xi}^{(n-1)}\in\mathcal{O}_{n-1}}\Delta_{n-1}(\bm{\xi}^{(n-1)})\int(2\pi)^{-\frac{K}{2}}\exp\left(f_{n-1}(\theta\big|\bm{\xi}^{(n-1)})+(\eta_{j}+\alpha_{k})^{\mathrm{T}}\theta\right)d\theta

\displaystyle-\sum_{\bm{\xi}^{(n-1)}\in\mathcal{O}_{n-1}}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\exp(\mu_{j})\left(\varphi+n\eta_{1}-\sum_{j=2}^{J}\xi_{j}^{(n)}(\eta_{1}-\eta_{j})\right)^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\eta}_{j}

\displaystyle\times\Delta_{n-1}(\bm{\xi}^{(n-1)})\int(2\pi)^{-\frac{K}{2}}\exp\left(f_{n-1}(\theta\big|\bm{\xi}^{(n-1)})+\eta_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta\right)d\theta.

(S.163)

By part (1) in Proposition
4
, there exists
\mathcal{H}_{\eta_{1}},\ldots,\mathcal{H}_{\eta_{J}}
corresponding to
\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{J}
. By excluding a zero measure in the parameter space, we assume WLOG that
\|P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{\eta_{1}}}\eta_{1}\|
achieves the unique maximum among
\|P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{\eta_{1}}}\eta_{1}\|,\ldots,\|P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{\eta_{J}}}\eta_{J}\|
. Similar to the proof in Theorem
1
, we divide the problem into two cases:

Case 1:
\|P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{\eta_{1}}}\eta_{1}\|>0
.

Then by similar method as in the proof of Theorem
1
, there exists linearly independent
\alpha_{k_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{k_{m}}
such that
\mathcal{H}_{\eta_{1}}=span\{\alpha_{k_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{k_{m}}\}
and
P_{\mathcal{H}_{\eta_{1}}}\eta_{1}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\gamma_{k_{j}}\alpha_{k_{j}}
. For notation simplicity, we denote the right hand side of (
S.3.2
) as

\displaystyle n\widetilde{\varphi}^{\mathrm{T}}\eta_{1}\phi_{n}(\mathbf{0})-n\sum_{j=1}^{m}\omega_{k_{j}}\widetilde{\alpha}_{k_{j}}^{\mathrm{T}}\eta_{1}\int(2\pi)^{-\frac{K}{2}}\exp\left(f_{n}(\theta\big|\bm{\xi}^{(n)})+\alpha_{k_{j}}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta\right)d\theta

\displaystyle-\widetilde{\mu}_{1}\phi_{n}(\mathbf{0})+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\omega_{k_{j}}\widetilde{\eta}_{1}^{\mathrm{T}}\alpha_{k_{j}}\int(2\pi)^{-\frac{K}{2}}\exp\left(f_{n}(\theta\big|\bm{\xi}^{(n)})+\alpha_{k_{j}}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta\right)d\theta-\left(\varphi+n\eta_{1}\right)^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\eta}_{1}\phi_{n}(\mathbf{0})+\mathcal{E}_{n}.

We can show that there exists constant
c>0
such that

\displaystyle\left|n\widetilde{\varphi}^{\mathrm{T}}\eta_{1}\phi_{n}(\mathbf{0})-n\sum_{j=1}^{m}\omega_{k_{j}}\widetilde{\alpha}_{k_{j}}^{\mathrm{T}}\eta_{1}\int(2\pi)^{-\frac{K}{2}}\exp\left(f_{n}(\theta\big|\mathbf{0})+\alpha_{k_{j}}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta\right)d\theta\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\widetilde{\mu}_{1}\phi_{n}(\mathbf{0})+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\omega_{k_{j}}\widetilde{\eta}_{1}^{\mathrm{T}}\alpha_{k_{j}}\int(2\pi)^{-\frac{K}{2}}\exp\left(f_{n}(\theta\big|\mathbf{0})+\alpha_{k_{j}}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta\right)d\theta-\left(\varphi+n\eta_{1}\right)^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\eta}_{1}\phi_{n}(\mathbf{0})\right|=\left|\mathcal{E}_{n}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\exp(-cn)\min_{j=1,\ldots,m}\left\{\int(2\pi)^{-\frac{K}{2}}\exp\left(f_{n}(\theta\big|\mathbf{0})+\alpha_{k_{j}}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta\right)d\theta\right\}\land\phi_{n}(\mathbf{0}).

Then by similar proof as in Theorem
1
, we expand

\displaystyle n\widetilde{\varphi}^{\mathrm{T}}\eta_{1}\phi_{n}(\mathbf{0})-n\sum_{j=1}^{m}\omega_{k_{j}}\widetilde{\alpha}_{k_{j}}^{\mathrm{T}}\eta_{1}\int(2\pi)^{-\frac{K}{2}}\exp\left(f_{n}(\theta\big|\mathbf{0})+\alpha_{k_{j}}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta\right)d\theta

\displaystyle-\widetilde{\mu}_{1}\phi_{n}(\mathbf{0})+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\omega_{k_{j}}\widetilde{\eta}_{1}^{\mathrm{T}}\alpha_{k_{j}}\int(2\pi)^{-\frac{K}{2}}\exp\left(f_{n}(\theta\big|\mathbf{0})+\alpha_{k_{j}}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta\right)d\theta-\left(\varphi+n\eta_{1}\right)^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\eta}_{1}\phi_{n}(\mathbf{0})

in infinite series. By matching finite terms in decreasing order whose order differences with the leading term are smaller then
\exp(cn)
, we can show if any of the following:
\widetilde{\varphi}^{\mathrm{T}}\eta_{1}-\widetilde{\eta}_{1}^{\mathrm{T}}\eta_{1}
,
\widetilde{\mu}_{1}+\varphi^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\eta}_{1}
,
\widetilde{\alpha}_{k_{j}}^{\mathrm{T}}\eta_{1},j=1,\ldots,m
and
{\alpha}_{k_{j}}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\eta}_{1},j=1,\ldots,m
is nonzero, by similar method as in the proof in Theorem
1
, there exists
l\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\displaystyle\left|n\widetilde{\varphi}^{\mathrm{T}}\eta_{1}\phi_{n}(\mathbf{0})-n\sum_{j=1}^{m}\omega_{k_{j}}\widetilde{\alpha}_{k_{j}}^{\mathrm{T}}\eta_{1}\int(2\pi)^{-\frac{K}{2}}\exp\left(f_{n}(\theta\big|\mathbf{0})+\alpha_{k_{j}}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta\right)d\theta\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\widetilde{\mu}_{1}\phi_{n}(\mathbf{0})+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\omega_{k_{j}}\widetilde{\eta}_{1}^{\mathrm{T}}\alpha_{k_{j}}\int(2\pi)^{-\frac{K}{2}}\exp\left(f_{n}(\theta\big|\mathbf{0})+\alpha_{k_{j}}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta\right)d\theta-\left(\varphi+n\eta_{1}\right)^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\eta}_{1}\phi_{n}(\mathbf{0})\right|

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle n^{-l}\min_{j=1,\ldots,m}\left\{\int(2\pi)^{-\frac{K}{2}}\exp\left(f_{n}(\theta\big|\mathbf{0})+\alpha_{k_{j}}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta\right)d\theta\right\}\land\phi_{n}(\mathbf{0}),

which leads to contradiction. Hence for any
j=1,\ldots,m
we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{\varphi}^{\mathrm{T}}\eta_{1}-\widetilde{\eta}_{1}^{\mathrm{T}}\eta_{1}=\widetilde{\mu}_{1}+\varphi^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\eta}_{1}=\widetilde{\alpha}_{k_{j}}^{\mathrm{T}}\eta_{1}={\alpha}_{k_{j}}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\eta}_{1}=0.

Then we use similar method as in the proof of Proposition
2
to rank all terms in the right hand side of (
S.3.2
) in decreasing order. By excluding a zero measure set in the parameter space, we can assume that there are no ties in the ranking. Then we can use similar method to show that each term dominates the summation of all terms with lower rank. Hence we can prove inductively that each term should be strictly equal to 0. By this method, we can prove that for any
j,j_{1},j_{2}=1,\ldots,J
and
k=1,\ldots,W
, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{\varphi}^{\mathrm{T}}\eta_{j}=\widetilde{\alpha}_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\eta_{j}={\alpha}_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\eta}_{j}=\eta_{j_{1}}^{\mathrm{T}}\eta_{j_{2}}=\widetilde{\mu}_{j}+\varphi^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\eta}_{j}=0.

Since
{\alpha}_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\eta}_{j}=0
for any
j=1,\ldots,J
and
k=1,\ldots,W
,
\varphi^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\eta}_{j}
is also equal to zero. So we have
\widetilde{\mu}_{j}=0
for any
j=1,\ldots,J
. Hence we finishes the prove on
[0,t_{q+1}]
.

Case 2:
\|P_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}_{\eta_{1}}}\eta_{1}\|=0
.

In this case,
\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{J}\in{X}\triangleq\{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\gamma_{k}\alpha_{k}:\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{K}\geq 0\}
by Proposition
4
. By Proposition
6
, for any
j=1,\ldots,J
, there exists canonical expansions for
\eta_{j}
under
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{K}
as:
\eta_{j}=\sum_{k=1}^{m_{j}}\gamma_{j,k}\alpha_{j,k}
, where the canonical expansion is unique in the sense that
\sum_{k=1}^{m_{j}}\gamma_{j,k}
is uniquely determined for each
j=1,\ldots,J
.

We assume WLOG that

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{m_{1}}\gamma_{1,k}

\displaystyle=\underset{j=1,\ldots,J}{\max}\sum_{k=1}^{m_{j}}\gamma_{j,k}.

We only consider the case where
\sum_{k=1}^{m_{1}}\gamma_{1,k}
is the unique largest term among
\sum_{k=1}^{m_{1}}\gamma_{1,k},\ldots,\sum_{k=1}^{m_{J}}\gamma_{J,k}
. By the proof in Theorem
1
, we can find concentration point
(\nu_{2},\ldots,\nu_{J})
for the summation
\sum_{\bm{\xi}^{(n)}\in\mathcal{O}_{n}}\Delta_{n}(\bm{\xi}^{(n)})\phi_{n}(\bm{\xi}^{(n)})
. We assume WLOG that
1>\nu_{2}\geq\ldots\geq\nu_{p}>\nu_{p+1}=\ldots=\nu_{J}=0
. We only consider the case where
\nu_{2},\ldots,\nu_{p}
are distinct. By the proof in Theorem
1
, we can construct unique characterization equation in the neighborhood of
(\nu_{2},\ldots,\nu_{J})
, where the solution of characterization equation has continuity property by Proposition
8
. Denote the solution of characterization equation by
\theta(\nu_{2},\ldots,\nu_{J})
at
(\nu_{2},\ldots,\nu_{J})
and denote the basis of characterization equation at
(\nu_{2},\ldots,\nu_{J})
by
\{\alpha_{j_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{j_{k}}\}
. Since
\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{J}\in span\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{W}\}
, by induction assumption we can easily seen that
\eta_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\alpha}_{k}=\eta_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{\varphi}=0
for any
j=1,\ldots,J
and
k=1,\ldots,W
.

By the construction method of characterization equation, after excluding a zero measure set in the parameter space, if there exists
1\leq\widetilde{p}\leq p
such that the expansion of
\eta_{\widetilde{p}}
in the equation is nondegenerated, then the expansions of
\eta_{\widetilde{p}+1},\ldots,\eta_{p}
are all nondegenerated. Moreover, the characterization equation contains type-1 equations only. For simplicity of proof, we consider the case where
p=2
. Moreover, we assume that the expansion of
\eta_{1}
is degenerated and the expansion of
\eta_{2}
is nondegenerated. Since
\eta_{1}
has degenerated expansion, by induction assumption we can easily show that
\widetilde{\eta}_{1}^{\mathrm{T}}\alpha_{k}=\widetilde{\eta}_{1}^{\mathrm{T}}\varphi=0
for any
k=1,\ldots,W
. Then by applying
t=t_{k}
in Proposition
9
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\bm{\xi}^{(n)}\in\mathcal{O}_{n}}\Delta_{n}(\bm{\xi}^{(n)})\sum_{j=1}^{J}\widetilde{\mu}_{j}\exp(\mu_{j})\int(2\pi)^{-\frac{K}{2}}\exp\left(f_{n}(\theta\big|\bm{\xi}^{(n)})+\eta_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta\right)d\theta

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{\bm{\xi}^{(n)}\in\mathcal{O}_{n}}\Delta_{n}(\bm{\xi}^{(n)})\sum_{j=1}^{J}\exp(\mu_{j})\int(2\pi)^{-\frac{K}{2}}\widetilde{\eta}_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta\exp\left(f_{n}(\theta\big|\bm{\xi}^{(n)})+\eta_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta\right)d\theta=0.

(S.164)

Since the expansion of
\eta_{1}
is degenerated, i.e.,
Z_{1}(t_{q+1})\in span\{Z_{1}(t_{1}),\ldots,Z_{1}(t_{q})\}
. Then by induction assumption we can easily show that
\widetilde{\eta}_{1}^{\mathrm{T}}\alpha_{k}=\widetilde{\eta}_{1}^{\mathrm{T}}\varphi=0
for any
k=1,\ldots,W
. Hence

\displaystyle\sum_{\bm{\xi}^{(n)}\in\mathcal{O}_{n}}\Delta_{n}(\bm{\xi}^{(n)})\exp(\mu_{1})\int(2\pi)^{-\frac{K}{2}}\widetilde{\eta}_{1}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta\exp\left(f_{n}(\theta\big|\bm{\xi}^{(n)})+\eta_{1}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta\right)d\theta=0.

Then we can prove that

\displaystyle-\sum_{\bm{\xi}^{(n)}\in\mathcal{O}_{n}}\Delta_{n}(\bm{\xi}^{(n)})\widetilde{\mu}_{1}\exp(\mu_{1})\int(2\pi)^{-\frac{K}{2}}\exp\left(f_{n}(\theta\big|\bm{\xi}^{(n)})+\eta_{1}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta\right)d\theta

dominates the left hand side of (
S.3.2
) if
\widetilde{\mu}_{1}\neq 0
, which leads to contradiction. Hence
\widetilde{\mu}_{1}=0
. Then (
S.3.2
) turns into

\displaystyle\sum_{\bm{\xi}^{(n)}\in\mathcal{O}_{n}}\Delta_{n}(\bm{\xi}^{(n)})\sum_{j=2}^{J}\widetilde{\mu}_{j}\exp(\mu_{j})\int(2\pi)^{-\frac{K}{2}}\exp\left(f_{n}(\theta\big|\bm{\xi}^{(n)})+\eta_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta\right)d\theta

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{\bm{\xi}^{(n)}\in\mathcal{O}_{n}}\Delta_{n}(\bm{\xi}^{(n)})\sum_{j=2}^{J}\exp(\mu_{j})\int(2\pi)^{-\frac{K}{2}}\widetilde{\eta}_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta\exp\left(f_{n}(\theta\big|\bm{\xi}^{(n)})+\eta_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta\right)d\theta=0

(S.165)

Following similar method as in the proof of Corollary
1
, by adding a
m
-th event type at the right end point we can similarly show that for any
m=2,\ldots,J
, there holds

\displaystyle-\sum_{\bm{\xi}^{(n)}\in\mathcal{O}_{n+1}}\Delta_{n+1}(\bm{\xi}^{(n+1)})\widetilde{\mu}_{m}\int(2\pi)^{-\frac{K}{2}}\exp\left(f_{n+1}(\theta\big|\bm{\xi}^{(n+1)})\right)d\theta

\displaystyle-\sum_{\bm{\xi}^{(n)}\in\mathcal{O}_{n+1}}\Delta_{n+1}(\bm{\xi}^{(n+1)})\int(2\pi)^{-\frac{K}{2}}\widetilde{\eta}_{m}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta\exp\left(f_{n+1}(\theta\big|\bm{\xi}^{(n+1)})\right)d\theta

\displaystyle+\sum_{\bm{\xi}^{(n)}\in\mathcal{O}_{n}}\Delta_{n}(\bm{\xi}^{(n)})\sum_{j=2}^{J}\widetilde{\mu}_{j}\exp(\mu_{j})\int(2\pi)^{-\frac{K}{2}}\exp\left(f_{n}(\theta\big|\bm{\xi}^{(n)})+(\eta_{j}+\eta_{m})^{\mathrm{T}}\theta\right)d\theta

\displaystyle+\sum_{\bm{\xi}^{(n)}\in\mathcal{O}_{n}}\Delta_{n}(\bm{\xi}^{(n)})\sum_{j=2}^{J}\exp(\mu_{j})\int(2\pi)^{-\frac{K}{2}}\widetilde{\eta}_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta\exp\left(f_{n}(\theta\big|\bm{\xi}^{(n)})+(\eta_{j}+\eta_{m})^{\mathrm{T}}\theta\right)d\theta=0.

(S.166)

If
\eta_{m}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta(\nu_{2},\ldots,\nu_{J})\neq 0
, by Proposition
10
we can show that

\displaystyle-\sum_{\bm{\xi}^{(n)}\in\mathcal{O}_{n+1}}\Delta_{n+1}(\bm{\xi}^{(n+1)})\int(2\pi)^{-\frac{K}{2}}\widetilde{\eta}_{m}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta\exp\left(f_{n+1}(\theta\big|\bm{\xi}^{(n+1)})\right)d\theta

dominates the summation on the left hand side of (
S.3.2
). Then by similar arguments as in the proof of Theorem
1
, this leads to contradiction. Hence
\eta_{m}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta(\nu_{2},\ldots,\nu_{J})=0
. Then we can show that

\displaystyle-\sum_{\bm{\xi}^{(n)}\in\mathcal{O}_{n+1}}\Delta_{n+1}(\bm{\xi}^{(n+1)})\widetilde{\mu}_{m}\int(2\pi)^{-\frac{K}{2}}\exp\left(f_{n+1}(\theta\big|\bm{\xi}^{(n+1)})\right)d\theta

dominates the left hand side of (
S.3.2
) if
\widetilde{\mu}_{m}\neq 0
, which leads to contradiction. Hence
\widetilde{\mu}_{m}=0
for any
m=1,\ldots,J
. Then equation (
S.3.2
) turns into

\displaystyle\sum_{\bm{\xi}^{(n)}\in\mathcal{O}_{n}}\Delta_{n}(\bm{\xi}^{(n)})\sum_{j=2}^{J}\exp(\mu_{j})\int(2\pi)^{-\frac{K}{2}}\widetilde{\eta}_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta\exp\left(f_{n}(\theta\big|\bm{\xi}^{(n)})+\eta_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta\right)d\theta=0.

(S.167)

By similar method as in the proof of Theorem
1
, we expand the left hand side of (
S.167
) in decreasing order. Since we need to prove that
\widetilde{\eta}_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}\alpha_{k}=0
for any
j=2,\ldots,J
and
k=1,\ldots,W
, we only need finite equations regarding all
\widetilde{\eta}_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}\alpha_{k}
after excluding a zero measure set in the parameter space. Hence there exists
l\in\mathbb{N}
such that we only need to match the coefficients of the terms with has order differences with the leading term which are less than
\exp(-l\log n)
. For
r\in\mathbb{N}
, denote
\widehat{\bf{\xi}}_{n}
and
\mathcal{A}_{r,n}
as

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{\xi}}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\underset{\bm{\xi}=(\xi_{2},\ldots,\xi_{J})\in\mathcal{E}_{k,n}:\xi_{3}=\ldots=\xi_{J}=0}{\operatorname{argmax}}~\Delta_{n}(\bm{\xi}){\phi}_{n}(\bm{\xi}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{r,n}

\displaystyle=\big\{\bm{\xi}^{(n)}=(\xi_{2}^{(n)},\ldots,\xi_{J}^{(n)}):|\xi_{2}^{(n)}-\widehat{\xi}_{2}^{(n)}|\leq n^{(\nu_{2}+\delta)/2},\sum_{j=3}^{J}\xi_{j}^{(n)}\leq r\big\},

where
\delta>0
is a constant small enough. By the proof in Theorem
1
, there exists
r^{\ast}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{\bm{\xi}^{(n)}\in\mathcal{O}_{n}\setminus\mathcal{A}_{r^{\ast},n}}\Delta_{n}(\bm{\xi}^{(n)})\sum_{j=2}^{J}\exp(\mu_{j})\int(2\pi)^{-\frac{K}{2}}\widetilde{\eta}_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta\exp\left(f_{n}(\theta\big|\bm{\xi}^{(n)})+\eta_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta\right)d\theta\leq\exp(-l\log n)\Delta_{n}(\widehat{\bm{\xi}}^{(n)}){\phi}_{n}(\widehat{\bm{\xi}}^{(n)}).

Hence we just need to expand all terms in

\displaystyle\sum_{\bm{\xi}^{(n)}\in\mathcal{A}_{r^{\ast},n}}\Delta_{n}(\bm{\xi}^{(n)})\sum_{j=2}^{J}\exp(\mu_{j})\int(2\pi)^{-\frac{K}{2}}\widetilde{\eta}_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta\exp\left(f_{n}(\theta\big|\bm{\xi}^{(n)})+\eta_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}\theta\right)d\theta

in decreasing order and match the coefficients of all terms which have order difference with
\Delta_{n}(\widehat{\bm{\xi}}^{(n)}){\phi}_{n}(\widehat{\bm{\xi}}^{(n)})
smaller than
\exp(-l\log n)
. Following the expansion method in
Shun and McCullagh, 1995
and similar method as in the proof of Theorem
1
, we can show that
\widetilde{\eta}_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}\alpha_{k}=0
for any
j=2,\ldots,J
and
k=1,\ldots,W
after excluding a zero measure set in the parameter space. Hence for any
j,j_{1},j_{2}=1,\ldots,W
and
k=1,\ldots,W
we have
\widetilde{\eta}_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}\alpha_{k}=\widetilde{\eta}_{j_{1}}^{\mathrm{T}}\eta_{j_{2}}=0
since
\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{J}\in span\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{W}\}
. This finishes the proof on
[0,t_{q+1}]
.

Hence by induction method, we prove that for any
j,j_{1},j_{2}
and
0<t,s<T
, there holds

\displaystyle u_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}X_{j}(t)

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle(V_{j_{1}}^{\mathrm{T}}Z_{j_{1}}(t))^{\mathrm{T}}(A_{j_{2}}^{\mathrm{T}}Z_{j_{2}}(s))

\displaystyle=0.

which indicates that
u_{j}=0
and
V_{j_{1}}A_{j_{2}}^{\mathrm{T}}=0
by Condition (d). Since there exists
D
rows among
A=(A_{1}^{\mathrm{T}},\ldots,A_{J}^{\mathrm{T}})^{\mathrm{T}}
which have full rank by Condition (c), we have
V_{j}=0
for any
j=1,\ldots,J
, which contradicts with the fact that
w
is nonzero. So we proved that
I(\alpha)
is finite and strictly positive definite at
\alpha=\alpha_{0}
.
∎

S.3.3
Proof of Proposition
9

Proof of Proposition
9
.

If
I(\delta_{0})
is singular, then there exists nonzero
w=\{u_{j0}\in\mathbb{R},u_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{L_{1}},V_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{L_{2}\times K}:j=1,\ldots,J\}
such that
\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\delta}\log L(\delta_{0}|N,X,Z)\right)^{\mathrm{T}}w=0
almost surely. Then it is easy to see that
\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\delta}L(\delta_{0}|N,X,Z)\right)^{\mathrm{T}}w=0
almost surely. For any
t\in[0,T]
, by integrating the above equation on
[t,T]
, we can see that
\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\delta}L(\delta_{0}|N,X,Z)\right)^{\mathrm{T}}w=0
still holds if
L(\delta_{0}|N,X,Z)
represents the likelihood function derived on interval
[0,t]
. By explicit calculation, we have

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\int\left[\sum_{j=1}^{J}\int_{0}^{t}(u_{j0}+u_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}X_{j}(s)+\theta^{\mathrm{T}}V_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}Z_{j}(s))dN_{j}(s)\right]\prod_{j=1}^{J}\Big[\prod_{s\leq t}(\lambda_{j}(s)^{\Delta N_{j}(s)})e^{-\int_{0}^{\mathrm{t}}\lambda_{j}(s)ds}\Big]\phi_{K}(\theta;0,I_{K})d\theta

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\int\left[\sum_{j=1}^{J}\int_{0}^{t}(u_{j0}+u_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}X_{j}(s)+\theta^{\mathrm{T}}V_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}Z_{j}(s))\lambda_{j}(s)ds\right]\prod_{j=1}^{J}\Big[\prod_{s\leq t}(\lambda_{j}(s)^{\Delta N_{j}(s)})e^{-\int_{0}^{\mathrm{t}}\lambda_{j}(s)ds}\Big]\phi_{K}(\theta;0,I_{K})d\theta~\text{a.s.}

(S.168)

For any fixed trajectory with positive density, there exists
t_{0}>0
small enough such that since
X_{j}
and
Z_{j}
are constant on
(t,t+t_{0})
and there are no events on
(t,t+t_{0})
. For any
0<\Delta t<t_{0}
, then we can derive

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\int\left[\sum_{j=1}^{J}\int_{0}^{t}(u_{j0}+u_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}X_{j}(s)+\theta^{\mathrm{T}}V_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}Z_{j}(s))dN_{j}(s)\right]\prod_{j=1}^{J}\Big[\prod_{s\leq t}(\lambda_{j}(s)^{\Delta N_{j}(s)})e^{-\int_{0}^{\mathrm{t}+\Delta t}\lambda_{j}(s)ds}\Big]\phi_{K}(\theta;0,I_{K})d\theta

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\int\left[\sum_{j=1}^{J}\int_{0}^{t+\Delta t}(u_{j0}+u_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}X_{j}(s)+\theta^{\mathrm{T}}V_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}Z_{j}(s))\lambda_{j}(s)ds\right]\prod_{j=1}^{J}\Big[\prod_{s\leq t}(\lambda_{j}(s)^{\Delta N_{j}(s)})e^{-\int_{0}^{\mathrm{t}+\Delta t}\lambda_{j}(s)ds}\Big]\phi_{K}(\theta;0,I_{K})d\theta.

(S.169)

By taking the
n
-th derivative of (
S.3.3
) with respect to
\Delta t
and let
\Delta t
go down to 0, we have

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\int\left[\sum_{j=1}^{J}\int_{0}^{t}(u_{j0}+u_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}X_{j}(s)+\theta^{\mathrm{T}}V_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}Z_{j}(s))(dN_{j}(s)-\lambda_{j}(s)ds)\right]

\displaystyle\times\prod_{j=1}^{J}\Big[\prod_{s\leq t}(\lambda_{j}(s)^{\Delta N_{j}(s)})e^{-\int_{0}^{\mathrm{t}}\lambda_{j}(s)ds}\Big]\left(\sum_{j=1}^{J}\lambda_{j}(t+0)\right)^{n}\phi_{K}(\theta;0,I_{K})d\theta

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\int\left[\sum_{j=1}^{J}(u_{j0}+u_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}X_{j}(t+0)+\theta^{\mathrm{T}}V_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}Z_{j}(t))\lambda_{j}(t)\right]

\displaystyle\times\prod_{j=1}^{J}\Big[\prod_{s\leq t}(\lambda_{j}(s)^{\Delta N_{j}(s)})e^{-\int_{0}^{\mathrm{t}}\lambda_{j}(s)ds}\Big]\left(\sum_{j=1}^{J}\lambda_{j}(t+0)\right)^{n-1}\phi_{K}(\theta;0,I_{K})d\theta.

For each
m\in\{1,\ldots,J\}
, by similar method as in the proof of Corollary
1
, we consider a hypothesized sample path on interval
[0,t+\Delta t]
which has same observed sample path on
[0,t+\Delta t)
but has the
m
-th event happening at time
t+\Delta t
. Then by differentiation, we have

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\int\left[\sum_{j=1}^{J}\int_{0}^{t}(u_{j0}+u_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}X_{j}(s)+\theta^{\mathrm{T}}V_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}Z_{j}(s))(dN_{j}(s)-\lambda_{j}(s)ds)+u_{m0}+u_{m}^{\mathrm{T}}X_{m}(t+0)+\theta^{\mathrm{T}}V_{m}^{\mathrm{T}}Z_{m}(t+0)\right]

\displaystyle\times\lambda_{m}(t+0)\prod_{j=1}^{J}\Big[\prod_{s\leq t}(\lambda_{j}(s)^{\Delta N_{j}(s)})e^{-\int_{0}^{\mathrm{t}}\lambda_{j}(s)ds}\Big]\left(\sum_{j=1}^{J}\lambda_{j}(t+0)\right)^{n}\phi_{K}(\theta;0,I_{K})d\theta

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\int\left[\sum_{j=1}^{J}(u_{j0}+u_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}X_{j}(t+0)+\theta^{\mathrm{T}}V_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}Z_{j}(t))\lambda_{j}(t)\right]

\displaystyle\times\lambda_{m}(t+0)\prod_{j=1}^{J}\Big[\prod_{s\leq t}(\lambda_{j}(s)^{\Delta N_{j}(s)})e^{-\int_{0}^{\mathrm{t}}\lambda_{j}(s)ds}\Big]\left(\sum_{j=1}^{J}\lambda_{j}(t+0)\right)^{n-1}\phi_{K}(\theta;0,I_{K})d\theta~~\text{a.s.}

∎

S.3.4
Proof of Proposition
10

To prove Proposition
10
, we first prove the following lemma:

Lemma 5
.

Let
\{f_{n}(x)\}
be a sequence of strictly concave functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
0
as their unique maximizers. Assume that
-\nabla^{2}f_{n}(x)\succeq\frac{1}{2}I_{d}
holds at any point
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
for any
n
. Let
\gamma,\{\beta_{n}\}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be
d
-vectors such that
\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{n}\rightarrow\infty
. Then for any
\delta>0
, for
n
large enough we have

\displaystyle 0<\frac{\left|\int_{x:\left\|x\right\|\geq C}\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}(x+\beta_{n})\exp(f_{n}(x))dx\right|}{\int_{x}\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}(x+\beta_{n})\exp(f_{n}(x))dx}\leq\delta,

where
C>0
is a constant which only depends on
\delta
.

Proof of Lemma
5
.

We change variable to
d
-dimensional polar coordinates:

\displaystyle\left|\int_{x:\left\|x\right\|\geq C}\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}(x+\beta_{n})\exp(f_{n}(x))dx\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{d-1}}\left(\prod_{k=2}^{d-1}\sin^{k-1}\theta_{k}\right)d\theta_{1}\ldots,d\theta_{d-1}\int_{r\geq C}r^{d-1}\left|\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}(r\alpha(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{d-1})+\beta_{n})\right|\exp(f_{n}(r\alpha(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{d-1})))dr

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{d-1}}\left(\prod_{k=2}^{d-1}\sin^{k-1}\theta_{k}\right)d\theta_{1}\ldots,d\theta_{d-1}\int_{r\geq C}r^{d-1}\left|r\|\gamma\|+|\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{n}|\right|\exp(f_{n}(r\alpha(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{d-1})))dr,

\displaystyle\int_{x:\left\|x\right\|\leq C}\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}(x+\beta_{n})\exp(f_{n}(x))dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{d-1}}\left(\prod_{k=2}^{d-1}\sin^{k-1}\theta_{k}\right)d\theta_{1}\ldots,d\theta_{d-1}\int_{r\geq C}r^{d-1}\left(\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}(r\alpha(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{d-1})+\beta_{n})\right)\exp(f_{n}(r\alpha(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{d-1})))dr

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\int_{\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{d-1}}\left(\prod_{k=2}^{d-1}\sin^{k-1}\theta_{k}\right)d\theta_{1}\ldots,d\theta_{d-1}\int_{r\leq C}r^{d-1}\left(|\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{n}|-C\|\gamma\|\right)\exp(f_{n}(r\alpha(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{d-1})))dr,

(S.170)

where
\left\|\alpha(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{d-1})\right\|=1
. For fixed
\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{d-1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
C>0
, we have

\displaystyle-\frac{d}{dr}\Bigg|_{r=C}f_{n}(r\alpha)=

\displaystyle-\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}\nabla f_{n}(r\alpha)\Big|_{r=C}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}\left(\nabla f_{n}(0)+r\nabla^{2}f_{n}(x_{r}^{\ast})\alpha\right)\Big|_{r=C}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}\left(-\nabla^{2}f_{n}(x_{r}^{\ast})\right)\alpha\Big|_{r=C}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}r\left\|\alpha\right\|^{2}\Big|_{r=C}=C/2

(S.171)

since
-\nabla^{2}f_{n}(x_{r}^{\ast})\succeq I_{d}
. Similarly we have

\displaystyle-\frac{d^{2}}{dr^{2}}\Bigg|_{r=C}f_{n}(r\alpha)=

\displaystyle-\alpha^{\mathrm{T}}\nabla^{2}f_{n}(r\alpha)\Big|_{r=C}\alpha\geq\frac{1}{2}\left\|\alpha\right\|^{2}=1/2.

(S.172)

We choose
C
large enough such that for any
r\geq C
, there holds:
\max\{r^{d-1}\exp\left(-\frac{C}{2}r-\frac{1}{4}r^{2}\right),\\
r^{d}\exp\left(-\frac{C}{2}r-\frac{1}{4}r^{2}\right)\}\leq(\frac{r}{2}+\frac{1}{4}C)\exp\left(-\frac{1}{4}Cr-\frac{1}{4}r^{2}\right)
. Then by (
5
) and (
S.172
) we have

\displaystyle\int_{r\geq C}r^{d-1}\left(r\|\gamma\|+|\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{n}|\right)\exp(f_{n}(r\alpha(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{d-1})))dr

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\exp(f_{n}(C\alpha))\int_{r\geq C}r^{d-1}\left(r\|\gamma\|+|\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{n}|\right)\exp(f_{n}(r\alpha(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{d-1})))dr

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\exp(f_{n}(C\alpha))(\|\gamma\|+|\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{n}|)\int_{r\geq C}\big(\frac{r}{2}+\frac{1}{4}C\big)\exp(-\frac{1}{4}Cr-\frac{1}{4}r^{2})dr

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\|\gamma\|+|\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{n}|)\exp(f_{n}(C\alpha)-\frac{1}{2}C^{2}).

(S.173)

On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle\int_{r\leq C}r^{d-1}\left(|\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{n}|-C\|\gamma\|\right)\exp(f_{n}(r\alpha(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{d-1})))dr

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\left(|\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{n}|-C\|\gamma\|\right)\exp(f_{n}(C\alpha))\int_{r\leq C}r^{d-1}dr

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{C^{d}\left(|\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{n}|-C\|\gamma\|\right)\exp(f_{n}(C\alpha))}{d}.

(S.174)

By (
5
), (
5
) and (
5
), for
n
large enough we have

\displaystyle 0<\frac{|\int_{x:\left\|x\right\|\geq C}\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}(x-\beta_{n})\exp(f_{n}(x))dx|}{\int_{x:\left\|x\right\|\leq C}\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}(x-\beta_{n})\exp(f_{n}(x))dx}\leq\frac{d(\|\gamma\|+|\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{n}|)\exp(-\frac{C^{2}}{2})}{C^{d}\left(|\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{n}|-C\|\gamma\|\right)}=\frac{d(1+\|\gamma\|/|\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{n}|)\exp(-\frac{C^{2}}{2})}{C^{d}\left(1-C\|\gamma\|/|\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{n}|\right)}.

Since
\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{n}\rightarrow\infty
, for any
\delta>0
, we can find
C
which only depends on
\delta
such that

\displaystyle 0<\frac{|\int_{x:\left\|x\right\|\geq C}\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}(x-\beta_{n})\exp(f_{n}(x))dx|}{\int_{x}\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}(x-\beta_{n})\exp(f_{n}(x))dx}\leq\delta

for any
n
large enough.
∎

Proof of Proposition
10
.

We only consider the case when
\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}_{n}\rightarrow\infty
. We apply Lemma
5
to the case when
\delta=\frac{1}{2}
and obtain the corresponding constant
C
. Since we have

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}\log(\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}\theta)=-\frac{1}{\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}\theta}\alpha\alpha^{\mathrm{T}},

which converges to 0 uniformly for
\|\theta-\widehat{\theta}_{n}\|\leq C
since
\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}_{n}\rightarrow\infty
. So for
n
large enough and any
\|\theta-\widehat{\theta}_{n}\|\leq C
, we have

\displaystyle I(\theta)-\frac{1}{2}I_{d}\leq\nabla^{2}(\log(\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}\theta)-f_{n}(\theta))\leq I(\theta)+I_{d}.

It is easy to see that for any
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\displaystyle\exp(-\max_{k=1,\ldots,K}\|\alpha_{k}\|\|\widehat{\theta}_{n}-\theta\|)(I(\widehat{\theta}_{n})-I_{d})\leq(I(\theta)-I_{d})\leq\exp(\max_{k=1,\ldots,K}\|\alpha_{k}\|\|\widehat{\theta}_{n}-\theta\|)(I(\widehat{\theta}_{n})-I_{d}).

(S.175)

Now let

\displaystyle g_{n,1}(\theta)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}(\theta-\widehat{\theta}_{n})^{\mathrm{T}}[\frac{1}{2}I_{d}+\exp(-C\max_{k=1,\ldots,K}\|\alpha_{k}\|)(I(\widehat{\theta}_{n})-I_{d})](\theta-\widehat{\theta}_{n})+f_{n}(\widehat{\theta}_{n})+\log(\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}_{n}),

\displaystyle g_{n,2}(\theta)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}(\theta-\widehat{\theta}_{n})^{\mathrm{T}}[2I_{d}+\exp(C\max_{k=1,\ldots,K}\|\alpha_{k}\|)(I(\widehat{\theta}_{n})-I_{d})](\theta-\widehat{\theta}_{n})+f_{n}(\widehat{\theta}_{n})+\log(\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}_{n})

be strictly concave function with maximizer
\widehat{\theta}_{n}
and maximum value
f(\widehat{\theta}_{n})
. Then for any
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\|\theta-\widehat{\theta}_{n}\|\leq C
, by (
S.175
) we have

\displaystyle-\nabla^{2}g_{n,1}(\theta)\leq\nabla^{2}(\log(\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}\theta)-f_{n}(\theta))\leq-\nabla^{2}g_{n,2}(\theta).

(S.176)

Since the maximizers and maximum values are matched for
f,g_{1},g_{2}
, by (
S.176
) we have

\displaystyle\int_{\theta:\|\theta-\widehat{\theta}_{n}\|\leq C}\exp(g_{n,2}(\theta))d\theta\leq\int_{\theta:\|\theta-\widehat{\theta}_{n}\|\leq C}(\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}\theta)\exp(f_{n}(\theta))d\theta\leq\int_{\theta:\|\theta-\widehat{\theta}_{n}\|\leq C}\exp(g_{n,1}(\theta))d\theta.

(S.177)

By the definition of
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
, it is easy to prove that
-\nabla^{2}g_{1}(\theta)\succeq I_{d}/2
and
-\nabla^{2}g_{2}(\theta)\succeq I_{d}/2
for any
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then by (
S.177
) and the choice of
C
, for
n
large enough, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\int(\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}\theta)\exp(f_{n}(\theta))d\theta}{\int\exp(g_{n,2}(\theta))d\theta}\geq\frac{\int_{\theta:\|\theta-\widehat{\theta}_{n}\|\leq C}(\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}\theta)\exp(f_{n}(\theta))d\theta}{2\int_{\theta:\|\theta-\widehat{\theta}_{n}\|\leq C}\exp(g_{n,2}(\theta))d\theta}\geq\frac{1}{2},

\displaystyle\frac{\int(\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}\theta)\exp(f_{n}(\theta))d\theta}{\int\exp(g_{n,1}(\theta))d\theta}\leq\frac{2\int_{\theta:\|\theta-\widehat{\theta}_{n}\|\leq C}(\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}\theta)\exp(f_{n}(\theta))d\theta}{\int_{\theta:\|\theta-\widehat{\theta}_{n}\|\leq C}\exp(g_{n,1}(\theta))d\theta}\leq 2.

(S.178)

Moreover, by the definition of
g_{n,1}
and
g_{n,2}
we have

\displaystyle\int(2\pi)^{-d/2}\exp(g_{n,1}(\theta))d\theta

\displaystyle=\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}_{n}\exp(f_{n}(\widehat{\theta}_{n}))[\operatorname{det}(\frac{1}{2}I_{d}+\exp(-C\max_{k=1,\ldots,K}\|\alpha_{k}\|)(I(\widehat{\theta}_{n})-I_{d}))]^{-1/2},

\displaystyle\int(2\pi)^{-d/2}\exp(g_{n,2}(\theta))d\theta

\displaystyle=\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}_{n}\exp(f_{n}(\widehat{\theta}_{n}))[\operatorname{det}(2I_{d}+\exp(C\max_{k=1,\ldots,K}\|\alpha_{k}\|)(I(\widehat{\theta}_{n})-I_{d}))]^{-1/2}.

(S.179)

Since
I(\widehat{\theta}_{n})\geq I_{d}
, there exists constant
C_{1},C_{2}>0
independent of
n
such that

\displaystyle[\operatorname{det}(\frac{1}{2}I_{d}+\exp(-C\max_{k=1,\ldots,K}\|\alpha_{k}\|)(I(\widehat{\theta}_{n})-I_{d}))]^{-1/2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}[\operatorname{det}(I(\widehat{\theta}_{n}))]^{-1/2},

\displaystyle[\operatorname{det}(2I_{d}+\exp(C\max_{k=1,\ldots,K}\|\alpha_{k}\|)(I(\widehat{\theta}_{n})-I_{d}))]^{-1/2}

\displaystyle\geq C_{2}[\operatorname{det}(I(\widehat{\theta}_{n}))]^{-1/2}.

(S.180)

Then by (
S.3.4
), (
S.3.4
) and (
S.3.4
), for
n
large enough we have

\displaystyle\frac{C_{2}}{2}\leq\frac{\int(2\pi)^{-d/2}(\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}\theta)\exp(f_{n}(\theta))d\theta}{\gamma^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\theta}_{n}\exp(f_{n}(\widehat{\theta}_{n}))/\sqrt{\operatorname{det}(I(\widehat{\theta}_{n}))}}\leq 2C_{1}.

Since constant
C_{1},C_{2}
does not depend on
n
, the result is proved.
∎

S.4
Proof of Theorem
3

Proof of Theorem
3
.

By the results in Theorem
1
, Theorem
2
and Condition (a), the consistency and asymptotic normality of
\widehat{\delta}_{n}
hold by standard maximum likelihood estimation argument.
∎

S.5
Proof of Theorem
4

Proof of Theorem
4
.

By Condition (a)-(e), the conditions (A) and (C) in
Fan and Li, 2001
are verified. By the result in Theorem
2
, condition (B) is also verified. Hence by similar proof as in
Fan and Li, 2001
, Theorem
4
is proved.
∎
